regular nodes on each side has a maximum matching among its nodes of size at least
2\cdot\left(1-B/2\right)n\left[1-(1-B/2)\alpha-e^{-2\alpha(1-B/2)}\right]-o(n)
. Correspondingly, a subgraph of
(1-B/2)n-n^{5/8}
regular nodes on each side has the same asymptotic maximum matching size (up to
o(n)
nodes that are removed).
Conditioning on
E_{1}

Lemma

4.2
thus implies that as
n\to\infty

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[n_{1}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[n_{1}|E_{1}\right]\mathbb{P}\left[E_{1}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[n_{1}|E_{1}^{c}\right]\mathbb{P}\left[E_{1}^{c}\right]\geq

\displaystyle\;\left(\mathbb{E}\left[m_{1}\right]-o(n)\right)\cdot\left(1-e^{-\Omega\left(n^{1/4}\right)}\right)-n^{5/8}\cdot e^{-\Omega\left(n^{1/4}\right)}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\;2\cdot\left(1-B/2\right)n\left[1-(1-B/2)\alpha-e^{-2\alpha(1-B/2)}\right]-o(n).

Through a similar conditioning, we find that:

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[\text{regular node }v\in V_{l}\text{ not connected to any regular node in }V_{r}\right]

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\;\lim_{n\to\infty}\left(1-2\alpha/n\right)^{(1-B/2)n+n^{5/8}}\cdot\left(1-e^{-\Omega\left(n^{1/4}\right)}\right)+0\cdot e^{-\Omega\left(n^{1/4}\right)}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\;\lim_{n\to\infty}\left(1-2\alpha/n\right)^{(1-B/2)\cdot n+n^{5/8}}=e^{-2\alpha(1-B/2)}.

Thus, as
n\to\infty
we can also upper bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[n_{1}\right]=

\displaystyle\;\mathbb{E}\left[n_{1}|E_{1}\right]\mathbb{P}\left[E_{1}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[n_{1}|E_{1}^{c}\right]\mathbb{P}\left[E_{1}^{c}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\;\left(1-e^{-2\alpha(1-B/2)}\right)\left((1-B/2)n+n^{5/8}\right)\cdot\left(1-e^{-\Omega\left(n^{1/4}\right)}\right)+n\cdot e^{-\Omega\left(n^{1/4}\right)}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\;\left(1-e^{-2\alpha(1-B/2)}\right)\cdot(1-B/2)\cdot n+o(n).

Now we examine the flexible nodes and argue that, even if we greedily match the flexible nodes to any unmatched regular nodes on the opposite side, almost all of the flexible nodes will be matched in the second stage. Given
n^{\prime}
unmatched regular nodes in
V_{r},
the number of edges between a flexible node
v\in V_{l}
and these
n^{\prime}
nodes is governed by
\text{Binom}\left(n^{\prime},\frac{\alpha^{f}+\alpha}{n}\right)
. For any
n^{\prime}\in\Theta(n),
by the Poisson Limit Theorem we find that
\text{Binom}\left(n^{\prime},\frac{\alpha^{f}+\alpha}{n}\right)
converges in distribution to
\text{Poisson}\left(\frac{n^{\prime}}{n}\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)\right)
as
n\to\infty.
Thus, as
n\to\infty

\mathbb{P}\left[v\text{ not connected to any unmatched regular node in }V_{r}|n^{\prime}\right]=e^{-\frac{n^{\prime}}{n}\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)}.

We now apply this bound to the flexible nodes in
V_{l}.
Observe that the event

E_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=E_{1}\cap\left\{n_{1}\leq\left(1-e^{-2\alpha(1-B/2)}\right)(1-B/2)n+2n^{5/8}\right\}

occurs with a probability of at least
1-e^{-\Omega\left(n^{1/4}\right)}
by Chernoff bound.
8
8

8

With high probability there are at most
(1-B/2)n+n^{5/8}
regular nodes on both sides; each such node is isolated with probability
e^{-2\alpha(1-B/2)}
, so we expect to match at most
\left((1-B/2)n+n^{5/8}\right)(1-e^{-2\alpha(1-B/2)})
of them in the first stage.
Under
E_{2}
, we greedily match each flexible node to any unmatched regular node in
V_{r}.
In particular, for the
i
th flexible node under consideration, even if all previous
i-1
flexible nodes are already matched to regular nodes in
V_{r},
there will still be at least

(1-B/2)\cdot n-\left(1-e^{-2\alpha(1-B/2)}\right)(1-B/2)n-i-3\cdot n^{5/8}

unmatched regular nodes in
V_{r}
. This allows us to bound:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\text{$i$th flexible node not connected to any unmatched regular node in }V_{r}|E_{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\;e^{-\frac{(1-B/2)\cdot n-\left(1-e^{-2\alpha(1-B/2)}\right)(1-B/2)n-i-3\cdot n^{5/8}}{n}\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)}\leq e^{-\left((1-B/2)-\left(1-e^{-2\alpha(1-B/2)}\right)(1-B/2)-i/n\right)\alpha^{f}}\text{ as }n\to\infty.

Thus, as
n\to\infty,
through the greedy algorithm that iteratively matches each flexible node to any unmatched regular node, the
i
th flexible node ends up matched with probability at least

1-e^{-\left((1-B/2)-\left(1-e^{-2\alpha(1-B/2)}\right)(1-B/2)-i/n\right)\alpha^{f}}=1-e^{-\left(-i/n+e^{-2\alpha(1-B/2)}(1-B/2)\right)\alpha^{f}}.

This is a lower bound on the matching probability for any
i\in\left\{1,2,...,B/2\cdot n-n^{5/8}\right\}
. The argument for matching flexible nodes in
V_{r}
with regular nodes in
V_{l}
is symmetric, and we find that:

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{n_{2}}{n}|E_{2}\right]\geq

\displaystyle 2\cdot\lim_{n\to\infty}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{B/2\cdot n-n^{5/8}}\left(1-e^{-\left(-i/n+e^{-2\alpha(1-B/2)}(1-B/2)\right)\alpha^{f}}\right)/n\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\cdot B/2-2\cdot\lim_{n\to\infty}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{B/2\cdot n}e^{-\left(-i/n+e^{-2\alpha(1-B/2)}(1-B/2)\right)\alpha^{f}}\right]/n

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle B-2\cdot\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{e^{\alpha^{f}(1/n-(1-B/2)e^{-2\alpha(1-B/2)})}\left(e^{\alpha^{f}B/2}-1\right)}{n\left(e^{\alpha^{f}/n}-1\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle B-2\cdot\frac{\lim_{n\to\infty}e^{\alpha^{f}(1/n-(1-B/2)e^{-2\alpha(1-B/2)})}\left(e^{\alpha^{f}B/2}-1\right)}{\lim_{n\to\infty}n\left(e^{\alpha^{f}/n}-1\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle B-2\cdot\frac{e^{-\alpha^{f}(1-B/2)e^{-2\alpha(1-B/2)}}\left(e^{\alpha^{f}B/2}-1\right)}{\lim_{n\to\infty}n\left(e^{\alpha^{f}/n}-1\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle B-\frac{2}{\alpha^{f}}\left[\left(e^{\alpha^{f}\cdot B/2}-1\right)e^{-\alpha^{f}(1-B/2)e^{-2\alpha(1-B/2)}}\right],

(11)

where the second equality comes from the sum of a geometric sequence, the third from the quotient rule, and the fifth from an application of the L’Hôpital’s rule on
\lim_{n\to\infty}\left(e^{\alpha^{f}/n}-1\right)/\left(1/n\right)
. Thus,

\displaystyle\mu(B/2,B/2)\geq

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{n_{1}+n_{2}}{n}\right]

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{n_{1}}{n}\right]+\lim_{n\to\infty}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{n_{2}}{n}|E_{2}\right]\cdot\mathbb{P}\left[E_{2}\right]\right)+0\cdot\lim_{n\to\infty}\left(1-\mathbb{P}\left[E_{2}\right]\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle 2\cdot\left(1-B/2\right)\left[1-(1-B/2)\alpha-e^{-2\alpha(1-B/2)}\right]

\displaystyle+B-\frac{2}{\alpha^{f}}\left[\left(e^{\alpha^{f}\cdot B/2}-1\right)e^{-\alpha^{f}(1-B/2)e^{-2\alpha(1-B/2)}}\right].

(12)

Combining (
10
) and (
12
), the condition for
\mu\left(B/2,B/2\right)>\mu(B,0)
is:

\begin{split}1-(1-B)\cdot e^{-2\alpha}<&2\cdot\left(1-B/2\right)\left[1-(1-B/2)\alpha-e^{-2\alpha(1-B/2)}\right]\\
&+B-\frac{2}{\alpha^{f}}\left[\left(e^{\alpha^{f}\cdot B/2}-1\right)e^{-\alpha^{f}(1-B/2)e^{-2\alpha(1-B/2)}}\right]\end{split}

(13)

Now we verify that the conditions stated in
Theorem

4.1
guarantee (
13
). Observe that the left-hand side of (
13
) equals
(1-B)\cdot\left(1-e^{-2\alpha}\right)+B
. Hence, (
13
) is equivalent to

\begin{split}\underbrace{(1-B)\cdot\left(1-e^{-2\alpha}\right)}_{(I)}+\underbrace{B}_{(II)}<&\underbrace{2\cdot\left(1-B/2\right)\left[1-(1-B/2)\alpha-e^{-2\alpha(1-B/2)}\right]}_{(III)}\\
&+\underbrace{B-\frac{2}{\alpha^{f}}\left[\left(e^{\alpha^{f}B/2}-1\right)e^{-\alpha^{f}(1-B/2)e^{-2\alpha(1-B/2)}}\right]}_{(IV)}.\end{split}

Thus, it suffices to compare
(I)
with
(III)
and
(II)
with
(IV)
separately.

For
(I)
and
(III)
, by Taylor expansion we use the elementary bounds

1-e^{-u}\leq u\quad\text{and}\quad e^{-u}\leq 1-u+u^{2}/2,\quad\forall u\geq 0.

Setting
x\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=1-B/2\in(1/2,1)
, we obtain

(I)\leq(1-B)\cdot 2\alpha=2\alpha(2x-1),\quad\text{and}\quad(III)\geq 2x\left(x\alpha-2\alpha^{2}x^{2}\right)=2\alpha x^{2}(1-2\alpha x).

Solving
(I)=(III)
yields

\alpha=0\text{ or }\alpha=\frac{(x-1)^{2}}{2x^{3}}=\frac{B^{2}}{8(1-B/2)^{3}}.

Thus, a sufficient condition for
(I)<(III)
is
\alpha\in\left(0,\frac{B^{2}}{8(1-B/2)^{3}}\right)
, i.e., imposing
\alpha\in\left(0,\frac{B^{2}}{8(1-B/2)^{3}}\right)
ensures

(III)-(I)\;\geq\;2\alpha\left[x^{2}(1-2\alpha x)-(2x-1)\right]>0.

Thus, to prove (
13
) under
\alpha\in\left(0,\frac{B^{2}}{8(1-B/2)^{3}}\right)
, it remains to show

(IV)-(II)>-2\alpha\left[x^{2}(1-2\alpha x)-(2x-1)\right].

\text{We first require}\qquad\alpha<\frac{1}{2(1-B/2)}\ln\left(\frac{2-B}{B}\right),

so that
xe^{-2\alpha x}>B/2
. This ensures that the exponential term
e^{\alpha^{f}B/2}\cdot e^{-\alpha^{f}(1-B/2)e^{-2\alpha(1-B/2)}}
in
(IV)
decreases with
\alpha^{f}
. Using the inequality
e^{u}-1\leq ue^{u}\;\forall u
, we find

(IV)\;\geq\;B-B\cdot e^{-\alpha^{f}\left(xe^{-2\alpha x}-B/2\right)}.

Therefore, a sufficient condition for
(IV)-(II)>-2\alpha\left[x^{2}(1-2\alpha x)-(2x-1)\right]
is

B\cdot e^{-\alpha^{f}\left(xe^{-2\alpha x}-B/2\right)}\;<\;2\alpha\left[x^{2}(1-2\alpha x)-(2x-1)\right].

\text{This condition is equivalent to}\quad\alpha^{f}\;>\;\alpha^{f}_{\star}(B,\alpha)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\frac{\ln\left(B\right)-\ln\left(2\alpha\left[\left(B/2\right)^{2}-2\alpha\left(1-B/2\right)^{3}\right]\right)}{\left(1-B/2\right)e^{-2\alpha\left(1-B/2\right)}-B/2}.

To conclude the proof we obtain
(I)+(II)<(III)+(IV)
by requiring

0<\alpha<\alpha_{\star}(B)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\min\left\{\frac{B^{2}}{8(1-B/2)^{3}},\;\frac{1}{2(1-B/2)}\ln\left(\frac{2-B}{B}\right)\right\}\quad\text{and}\quad\alpha^{f}>\alpha^{f}_{\star}(B,\alpha).\square

D.5
Analysis Based on the KS Algorithm

We begin with the definition of the KS algorithm in
Algorithm

1
. Intuitively, the KS algorithm proceeds in two phases: in the first phase, it repeatedly matches and removes nodes of degree
1
until no such nodes remain; in the second phase, it randomly selects edges to match until all edges are pruned. This algorithm has two advantages: first, its simple “greedy” structure makes it amenable to theoretical study, and prior works have described its behavior on random graphs. Second, it can be optimal in sparse settings. For example, it is always optimal if the graph is a tree and yields asymptotically optimal matchings for some classes of sparse random graphs
[
Karp and Sipser, 1981
,
Balister and Gerke, 2015
]
. These two facts combined make it a valuable tool to study maximum matching in sparse settings. Though prior works do not encapsulate the random graphs we study (see Section
1.3
), we show that the KS algorithm is amenable to our model.

Algorithm 1

Karp-Sipser’s (KS) Algorithm

1:

Input:
Graph
G

2:

Initialize graph
G^{\prime}
as an empty graph on the same set of nodes as
G

3:

while

G
has edges
do

4:

if
there exists a node of degree 1 in
G

then

5:

Choose an edge
e
that is incident to a node of degree 1 uniformly at random

6:

else

7:

Choose an edge
e
from all remaining edges uniformly at random

8:

Add edge
e
to graph
G^{\prime}

9:

Delete edge
e
and all edges incident to
e
from graph
G

10:

Output:
The number of edges in
G^{\prime}

The KS-based analysis underlies two key results of our paper. First, it allows us to analytically compare one-sided and balanced flexibility allocations in the parameter regimes identified in
Theorem

D.6
. Second, it motivates the study of
\mu^{\texttt{KS}}(b_{l},b_{r})
as a proxy performance measure and facilitates the characterization of its structural properties at
\mathbf{b}=(1/2,1/2)
in
Theorem

C.1
.

The rest of this section proceeds as follows. In Appendix
D.5.1
, we start by constructing a quantity
\mu^{\texttt{KS}}(b_{l},b_{r})
based on analyses of the KS algorithm. Under
Condition

1
,
Theorem

D.3
shows that
\mu^{\texttt{KS}}(b_{l},b_{r})
is equal to
\mu(b_{l},b_{r})
. We then verify
Condition

1
for a subset of instances, leading to
Theorem

D.5
, which establishes the equivalence of
\mu(b_{l},b_{r})
and
\mu^{\texttt{KS}}(b_{l},b_{r})
over a range of parameters. However, to overcome the additional complexity in comparing different flexibility allocations, we need to leverage computer-aided proofs. We show that
\mu^{\texttt{KS}}(b_{l},b_{r})
can be approximated to arbitrary precision by analyzing a system of non-linear equations that underpins this quantity. This enables us to compute
\mu^{\texttt{KS}}(b_{l},b_{r})
—and hence
\mu(b_{l},b_{r})
—at a provable level of accuracy and compare different allocations in
Theorem

D.6
. We then provide the proofs of all the results in Appendices
D.5.2
-
D.5.5
.

D.5.1
KS Derivations.

Throughout the section we fix an arbitrary
(b_{l},b_{r})
and use
G
as the shorthand notation for
G^{glb}_{n}(b_{l},b_{r})
. In
G
, the
degree
of a node
v
is the number of edges that are incident to
v
, and we denote this number by
deg(v)
.

Based on
Algorithm

1
, the edges in
G^{\prime}
form a matching; our goal will be to characterize the size of this matching. When all edges incident to a node
v
are deleted from
G
and yet
v
has degree
0
in
G^{\prime}
, we know that
v
will not be part of the resulting matching and call it an
isolated
node. The key to finding the size of a matching based on the KS algorithm is to count the number of nodes that either become matched or isolated as edges are deleted from the graph. We denote the iterations before the first occurrence where no nodes have a degree of 1 in
G
as
Phase 1
of the KS algorithm. The subsequent iterations are referred to as
Phase 2
of the KS algorithm. A key property of the KS algorithm is that it is optimal in its handling of degree-
1
vertices: given an edge
e
that is incident to a degree-
1
vertex, there is always a maximum matching that contains
e

[
Bohman and Frieze, 2011
,
Balister and Gerke, 2015
]
. This result implies that the KS algorithm is optimal until the end of phase 1. Let
M^{l}_{1},M^{r}_{1},M^{l}_{2}
and
M^{r}_{2}
respectively denote the set of nodes in
V_{l}
and
V_{r}
that enter the matching (i.e., incident to edges in
G^{\prime}
) during phase 1 and 2. By symmetry, we know that
m_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=|M^{l}_{1}|=|M^{r}_{1}|
and
m_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=|M^{l}_{2}|=|M^{r}_{2}|
. Similarly, let
\Psi^{l}_{1},\Psi^{r}_{1},\Psi^{l}_{2}
and
\Psi^{r}_{2}
respectively represent the set of nodes that become isolated in
V_{l}
and
V_{r}
during phase 1 and 2, where
\Psi^{l}_{1}
and
\Psi^{r}_{1}
also include the nodes that are already isolated initially in graph
G
. We define
\psi_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\max\{|\Psi^{l}_{1}|,|\Psi^{r}_{1}|\}
and

\psi_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=n-m_{1}-m_{2}-\psi_{1}=\min\left\{n-|M^{l}_{1}|-|M^{l}_{2}|-|\Psi^{l}_{1}|,n-|M^{r}_{1}|-|M^{r}_{2}|-|\Psi^{r}_{1}|\right\}=\min\{|\Psi^{l}_{2}|,|\Psi^{r}_{2}|\}.

Intuitively,
\psi_{2}
represents the number of nodes that become isolated in Phase 2 of the KS algorithm (excluding those already accounted for in Phase 1). It has been demonstrated, for some types of sparse random graph settings, that the expected number of nodes becoming isolated in Phase 2 of the KS algorithm is
o(n)
, i.e.,
\mathbb{E}\left[\psi_{2}\right]\in o(n)
. We state this as
Condition

1
, which we later verify for some instances of our model.

Condition 1

When the KS algorithm is applied to a random graph
G
of our model,
\mathbb{E}\left[\psi_{2}\right]\in o(n)
.

As the KS algorithm is optimal in Phase 1,
Condition

1
guarantees that it is asymptotically optimal. In particular, in Phase 2, the expected fraction of nodes that become isolated, and thus unmatched, is vanishingly small, i.e., it involves
o(n)
nodes. Thus, to identify the number of unmatched nodes in both phases, it suffices to evaluate
\psi_{1}
, for which we evaluate the probability of a node becoming isolated in Phase 1 of the KS algorithm. For
\mathbf{b}=(b_{l},b_{r}),
we will show that the following set of equations determines this probability:

\begin{split}w_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})=&e^{-2b_{r}\alpha^{f}\left(1-\hat{w}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\right)-(1-b_{r})\cdot(\alpha^{f}+\alpha)\left(1-\hat{w}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)},\\
w_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})=&e^{-b_{r}\cdot(\alpha^{f}+\alpha)\left(1-\hat{w}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\right)-2(1-b_{r})\alpha\left(1-\hat{w}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)},\\
w_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})=&1-e^{-2b_{r}\alpha^{f}\hat{w}_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})-(1-b_{r})(\alpha^{f}+\alpha)\hat{w}_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})},\\
w_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})=&1-e^{-b_{r}(\alpha^{f}+\alpha)\hat{w}_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})-2(1-b_{r})\alpha\hat{w}_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})},\\
\hat{w}_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})=&e^{-2b_{l}\alpha^{f}\left(1-w_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\right)-(1-b_{l})(\alpha^{f}+\alpha)\left(1-w_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)},\\
\hat{w}_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})=&e^{-b_{l}(\alpha^{f}+\alpha)\left(1-w_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\right)-2(1-b_{l})\alpha\left(1-w_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)},\\
\hat{w}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})=&1-e^{-2b_{l}\alpha^{f}w_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})-(1-b_{l})(\alpha^{f}+\alpha)w_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})},\\
\hat{w}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})=&1-e^{-b_{l}(\alpha^{f}+\alpha)w_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})-2(1-b_{l})\alpha w_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})}.\end{split}

(14)

Such equations are common in KS-based analyses: whereas Karp and Sipser’s original analysis focused on a homogeneous Erdős–Rényi graph and thus relied on just 2 of these equations, ours requires
8
equations to characterize the probability for flexible or regular nodes on either side to be isolated or not. We denote the smallest set of solutions
9
9

9

In line with terminology in
Karp and Sipser [1981]
, the smallest set of solutions refers to the least fixed point of the system of equations in (
14
). Note that this is well defined since all variables in
\mathbf{w}
are increasing functions of each other.

\mathbf{w}=\left(w_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b}),w_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b}),w_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b}),w_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b}),\hat{w}_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b}),\hat{w}_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b}),\hat{w}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b}),\hat{w}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)

to (
14
) by
\mathbf{y}=\left(y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b}),y_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b}),y_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b}),y_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b}),\hat{y}_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b}),\hat{y}_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b}),\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b}),\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right).

Theorem D.3

Let

\begin{split}\xi(b_{l},b_{r})=2&-b_{l}y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})-b_{r}\left(1-\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\right)\\
&-b_{r}\left(1-\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\right)\left(2b_{l}\alpha^{f}y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+(1-b_{l})(\alpha^{f}+\alpha)y_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)\\
&-(1-b_{l})y_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})-(1-b_{r})\left(1-\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)\\
&-(1-b_{r})\left(1-\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)\left(b_{l}(\alpha^{f}+\alpha)y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+2(1-b_{l})\alpha y_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right),\end{split}

(15)

\begin{split}\hat{\xi}(b_{l},b_{r})=2&-b_{r}\hat{y}_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})-b_{l}\left(1-y_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\right)\\
&-b_{l}\left(1-y_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\right)\left(2b_{r}\alpha^{f}\hat{y}_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+(1-b_{r})(\alpha^{f}+\alpha)\hat{y}_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)\\
&-(1-b_{r})\hat{y}_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})-(1-b_{l})\left(1-y_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)\\
&-(1-b_{l})\left(1-y_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)\left(b_{r}(\alpha^{f}+\alpha)\hat{y}_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+2(1-b_{r})\alpha\hat{y}_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right).\end{split}

(16)

Define
\mu^{\texttt{KS}}(b_{l},b_{r})=\min\left(\xi(b_{l},b_{r}),\hat{\xi}(b_{l},b_{r})\right).
Then, under
Condition

1
,
\mu(b_{l},b_{r})=\mu^{\texttt{KS}}(b_{l},b_{r}).

We now loosen
Condition

1
into a sufficient (not necessary) condition that is easier to verify.

Lemma D.4

Condition

1
holds when the solution to (
14
) is unique.

Lemma

D.4
is instrumental for
Theorem

D.5
below. It states that, when
B=1
,
\mu(b_{l},b_{r})
equals
\mu^{\texttt{KS}}(b_{l},b_{r})
for the one-sided and balanced allocations in almost all of the
subcritical regime
, classically defined
[
Aronson et al., 1998
]
as the setting where the average expected degree of a node,
\alpha^{f}+\alpha
in our case, is smaller than Euler’s number
e
.

Theorem D.5

When
10^{-4}<\alpha<\alpha^{f},\alpha^{f}+\alpha<e,
and
\mathbf{b}=(1,0)
or
(1/2,1/2)
,
\mu(b_{l},b_{r})=~\mu^{\texttt{KS}}(b_{l},b_{r}).

Though
Theorem

D.5
characterizes a region in which
\mu(\cdot,\cdot)=\mu^{\texttt{KS}}(\cdot,\cdot)
, this does not suffice to make formal comparisons between
\mu(1,0)
and
\mu(1/2,1/2)
; since there are no closed-form solutions to these nonlinear equations, we need to solve (
14
) to provable numerical precision for the region specified by the theorem (see (
28
) in Appendix
D.5.3
). This then allows us to compare
\mu(1,0)
and
\mu(1/2,1/2)
for these values of
\alpha^{f}
and
\alpha
. Moreover, we derive a continuity property of
\mu^{\texttt{KS}}
in
\alpha^{f}
and
\alpha
, that lets us construct local lower bounds for
\mu(1,0)-\mu(1/2,1/2)
(see (
26
) and (
27
)). We conclude by verifying in a computer-aided proof that these lower bounds exceed
0
across the parameters specified in the following theorem:

Theorem D.6

\mu(1,0)>\mu(1/2,1/2)
when (i)
\alpha^{f}+\alpha<e
and (ii)
10^{-4}<\alpha<0.77\alpha^{f}-0.16
.
10
10

10

The boundary in condition (ii) arises from the ability for a computer-aided proof to verify the inequality within a reasonable runtime: for
\delta>0
, we construct and compute a lower bound the value of
\mu(1,0)-\mu(1/2,1/2)
within each set of
[\alpha^{f},\alpha^{f}+\delta)\times[\alpha,\alpha+\delta)
within the subcritical regime. Taking
\delta=0.001
yields the boundary in
Theorem

D.6
(ii) and runs in about
20
hours.

Theorem

D.6
allows us to prove the dominance of the one-sided allocation for a wider set of parameters in which
\alpha^{f}+\alpha
remains relatively small (recall that
Theorem

3.1
allows for arbitrarily large
\alpha^{f}
but requires
\alpha=0
). Beyond the subcritical regime, KS-style analyses have fundamental limitations for two reasons: first, the asymptotic optimality of KS is not known for bipartite graphs beyond the subcritical regime (see
Bollobás and Brightwell [1995]
,
Mastin and Jaillet [2013]
); and secondly, our computer-aided comparison of different flexibility allocations requires the nonlinear equations to have a unique set of solutions, which is known
[
Karp and Sipser, 1981
, Lemma 1]
to require the subcritical regime.

Remark D.7

An alternative approach to characterizing the expected maximum matching size
\mu
is based on the analysis of a Monomer–Dimer model in the zero-temperature limit
z\to 0
, which leads to recursive distributional equations (RDEs)
[
Bordenave and Lelarge, 2010
,
Bordenave et al., 2013
]
. In this framework, there is a one-to-one correspondence between solutions to the RDEs and the “historical records” of a suitably defined function
F(t)
. The value of
\mu
can then be expressed explicitly in terms of
\max_{t\in[0,1]}F(t)
(see Theorems 2 and 3 of
Bordenave et al. [2013]
), thereby extending KS-style results to the supercritical regime. However, explicitly computing
\mu
via this approach requires analyzing the first-order condition of
F(t)
, which admits multiple solutions in the supercritical regime; only the smallest of these solutions corresponds to the global maximizer of
F(t)
. This procedure recovers the KS-style formulas for
G(n,c/n)
(see
Bordenave et al. [2013]
), but at the cost of substantial analytical complexity. For the purpose of explicitly computing
\mu
in our setting, we thus focus on the KS-style equations rather than the RDE-based approach.

The proofs of the KS-based results are structured as follows:

•

In Appendix
D.5.2
we prove
Theorem

D.3
,
Lemma

D.4
, and
Theorem

D.5
. This requires us to first state the definitions and auxiliary results commonly associated with KS-style analyses, and we then prove each of these three results.

•

In Appendix
D.5.3
we provide our computer-aided proof of
Theorem

D.6
. Our proof partitions the region of interest into small cells and numerically derives lower bounds on
\mu(1,0)
and upper bounds on
\mu(1/2,1/2)
across each such cell. For each cell, we identify a particular point
(\alpha,\alpha^{f})
at which we numerically solve (
14
) for both
\mathbf{b}=(1,0)
and
\mathbf{b}=(1/2,1/2)
within a given tolerance
\epsilon
(see
Claim

3
). Crucially, that tolerance guarantees that the solution to (
14
) is within
\epsilon
of the true solution; we analytically translate this bound into a bound on the gap between the numerically computed value and
\mu^{\texttt{KS}}
. We also show that the smallest solution to (
14
) is continuous in
\alpha
and
\alpha^{f}
, which implies that the bound holds, with an additional error term, within a
\delta
-neighborhood of
(\alpha,\alpha^{f})
, which represents the cell that
(\alpha,\alpha^{f})
is part of (
Claim

4
). We iterate over cells to verify for the entire region that our lower bounds on
\mu(1,0)
are greater than our upper bounds on
\mu(1/2,1/2)
.

•

In Appendix
D.5.4
we provide the computer-aided proof of
Theorem

C.1
. We take directional second-order derivatives (SOD) of
\mu^{\texttt{KS}}(b_{l},b_{r})
and evaluate them at
\mathbf{b}=(1/2,1/2)
to prove concavity and convexity results in the respective directions. In particular, the SODs depend only on
\alpha,\alpha^{f}
, and solutions to (
14
). Then, similar to the approach in Appendix
D.5.2
, we lower and upper bound the solution to (
14
) within every small cell and iterate over cells to verify the signs of the directional SODs.

•

In Appendix
D.5.5
we provide the proof of the auxiliary results in Appendix
D.5.2
.

D.5.2
Phase 1 of the KS Algorithm

In this section, we analyze phase 1 of the KS algorithm for
G^{glb}_{n}(b_{l},b_{r})
, hereafter referred to as
G
for notational simplicity. The set of edges in
G
is denoted by
E
, and the set of nodes is denoted by
V\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=V_{l}\cup V_{r}
. Our analysis extends the results for sparse random graphs presented in
Karp and Sipser [1981]
to random bipartite graphs. Similar to
Balister and Gerke [2015]
, we analyze bipartite graphs in which the degree distributions for nodes are heterogeneous; however, the “configuration model” considered in their paper does not capture our setting with flexible and regular nodes, and we require different probabilistic computations to handle the heterogeneous edge probabilities between nodes of different flexibility types (i.e.,
2\alpha
between two regular nodes,
\alpha^{f}+\alpha
between a flexible and a regular node, and
2\alpha^{f}
between two flexible nodes). We next present all auxiliary results needed for
Theorem

D.3
,
Lemma

D.4
and
Theorem

D.5
, the three main technical results based on KS-style analyses.

We begin by introducing the concept of a
derivation
, which is essential for computing the asymptotic size of a maximum matching. We shall show that nodes that appear in a derivation become either matched or isolated in Phase 1 of the KS algorithm. Moreover, depending on their positions in the derivation, nodes can be classified as either a
target
or a
loser
, which determines the number of nodes that become matched or isolated in Phase 1.

Definition D.8

A derivation is a sequence
a_{1},b_{1},a_{2},b_{2},...,
of distinct nodes such that, for
i=1,2,...\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}

(1)

\left\{a_{i},b_{i}\right\}\in E
;

(2)

\left\{a_{i},b\right\}\in E
implies
b\in\left\{b_{1},b_{2},...,b_{i}\right\}.

For example, the sequence
a_{1},b_{1},a_{2},b_{2},a_{3}
in
Fig.

30
(a) is a derivation: node
a_{1}
fulfills condition (1) because
\left\{a_{1},b_{1}\right\}\in E
, and fulfills condition (2) because it is connected to no other node. Next, we verify that
a_{2}
fulfills condition (1) because
\left\{a_{2},b_{2}\right\}\in E
, and fulfills condition (2) because it is only connected to
b_{1}
and
b_{2}.
The sequence ends with node
a_{3},
which is again only connected to
b_{1}
and
b_{2}.
On the other hand, in
Fig.

30
(b) there is no derivation involving the nodes
\{a_{1},a_{2},b_{1},b_{2}\}
: condition (2) implies that a node cannot appear as
a_{i}
unless all but one of its neighbors have already appeared in the derivation; in
Fig.

30
(b), for
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
there exists no ordering of
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
such that this is true (and vice versa for
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
).

In the upcoming proofs we will demonstrate that by following the KS algorithm one can optimally match nodes that appear in a derivation by starting with nodes of degree
1
and then iteratively resolving the remaining nodes.

(a)

(b)

Figure 30:
In
Fig.

30
(a), the sequence
a_{1},b_{1},a_{2},b_{2},a_{3}
is a derivation. Notice that all nodes in this derivation are either matched or isolated in phase
1
of the KS algorithm: the edge
(a_{1},b_{1})
is added to graph
G^{\prime}
as
a_{1}
is the only node of degree
1
within the connected component; we then delete all edges incident to
a_{1}
and
b_{1}
, leaving
(a_{2},b_{2})
and
(b_{2},a_{3})
as the only remaining edges in the graph; next, by adding
(a_{2},b_{2})
into
G^{\prime}
(the case of
(b_{2},a_{3})
is symmetric as the sequence
a_{1},b_{1},a_{3},b_{2},a_{2}
is also a derivation),
a_{1},b_{1},a_{2}
and
b_{2}
become matched while
a_{3}
becomes isolated. In contrast, there is no derivation in
Fig.

30
(b) that involves the connected component
\{a_{1},b_{1},a_{2},b_{2}\}
and none of these nodes become matched or isolated during phase 1 of the KS algorithm.

Within a derivation, we categorize nodes into
target
and
loser
based on the following definition:

Definition D.9

We define the following relation
\bigotimes\subseteq V\times V
:
v\bigotimes u
if there exists a derivation
a_{1},b_{1},a_{2},b_{2},...
and an index
i
such that
v=a_{i}
and
u=b_{i}.
We call
u
a target if for some
v,v\bigotimes u,
and we call
u
a loser if (1) for some
v,u\bigotimes v
or (2)
u
is the last element of an odd length derivation.

Based on
Definition

D.9
, all members of derivations are targets or losers or both. For instance, in the derivation
a_{1},b_{1},a_{2},b_{2},a_{3}
in
Fig.

30
(a),
a_{1},a_{2}
and
a_{3}
are losers while
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
are targets. The next result characterizes the nodes that are
processed
, i.e., that become either matched or isolated in phase 1 of the KS algorithm. The result is an immediate application of Theorem
8
in
Karp and Sipser [1981]
to bipartite graphs. We defer the proofs of
Proposition

D.10
and all auxiliary results in this section to Appendix
D.5.5
.

Proposition D.10

(Theorem
8
in
Karp and Sipser [1981]
)

Consider any execution of the KS algorithm on
G.
Denote by
M_{1}
the set of edges
(v,u)
that are added to
G^{\prime}
in Phase
1.
Then:

(i)

a node
v
is processed in phase 1 iff
v
occurs in some derivation;

(ii)

if
u
is a target then
M_{1}
contains exactly one edge
(v,u)
such that
v\bigotimes u;

(iii)

if edge
(v,u)\in M_{1}
then
v\bigotimes u
or
u\bigotimes v;

(iv)

if
v\bigotimes u
and
u\bigotimes v
then edge
(v,u)\in M_{1}
;

(v)

\psi_{1}=\max\left(\left|\left\{v\in V_{l}\mid v\text{ is a loser}\right\}\right|-\left|\left\{v\in V_{r}\mid v\text{ is a target}\right\}\right|,\left|\left\{v\in V_{r}\mid v\text{ is a loser}\right\}\right|-\left|\left\{v\in V_{l}\mid v\text{ is a target}\right\}\right|\right).

Thus, the key to finding
\mathbb{E}\left[\psi_{1}\right]
and the asymptotic matching probability lies in determining the probability of a node
v
being a target and/or a loser. We provide asymptotic answers to these questions by (1) conducting a probabilistic analysis of derivations in random trees and (2) demonstrating that a random tree is a good approximation to the structure obtained by selecting a node
v
in
G
and conducting a breadth-first search from
v
.

We now construct a random tree
\bar{G}_{n}(b_{l},b_{r})
to approximate the structure obtained from a breadth-first search from a node
v
in
G
. As illustrated in
Fig.

31
, we construct the layers of the tree sequentially, mimicking a breadth-first search from the root node. The flexibility types of all nodes in a given layer are sampled according to the same distribution, and the distribution for each layer alternates between
\text{Bernoulli}(b_{l})
or
\text{Bernoulli}(b_{r})
. This mimics the alternation between nodes in
V_{l}
and
V_{r}
in the bipartite graph
G
. Specifically, the construction of
\bar{G}_{n}(b_{l},b_{r})
follows a branching process: assume for simplicity that the flexibility type of the root node
v
is drawn from the
\text{Bernoulli}(b_{l})
distribution, so that
v
is a flexible node (i.e.,
F_{v}=1
) with probability
b_{l}
and a regular node with probability
1-b_{l}
. Then,
v
has
n
potential children, each being a flexible node (i.e.,
F_{u}=1
) with probability
b_{r}
and a regular node with probability
1-b_{r}
. A potential child becomes a realized child of
v
with probability
2p_{n}+(F_{v}+F_{u})\cdot(p^{f}_{n}-p_{n})
. Each realized child
u
then has
n
potential children, with flexible and regular probabilities of
b_{l}
and
1-b_{l}
, respectively. This branching process continues until no further child exists for a tree layer, a process that can be either finite or infinite. We omit the dependency on
n
and
\mathbf{b}
in
\bar{G}_{n}(b_{l},b_{r})
whenever it is clear from the context.

Figure 31:
Illustration of the branching process for
\bar{G}

To analyze the structure of
\bar{G}
and connect it to
G
, we define two subsets of nodes,
L
and
H
, through the procedure outlined in
Algorithm

2
. The set
L_{d}
contains all nodes added into set
L
in the first
d
repetitions of line 5 of Algorithm
2
; similarly, we denote by
H_{d}
the nodes added into set
H
in the first
d
repetitions of line 4 of Algorithm
2
. Since
L
contain all leaves of
\bar{G}
and all other nodes in
\bar{G}
have at least one child, every node in
\bar{G}
is added into either
H
or
L.
The classification of nodes into sets
L
and
H
is crucial for our study because it determines whether a node
v
is a target, a loser, or both, as stated in
Lemma

D.11
.

Algorithm 2

Classification of Nodes in
\bar{G}

1:

Input:
A random tree
\bar{G}
rooted at
v
.

2:

Initialize:

L
=
\{\text{Nodes in $\bar{G}$ with no children in $\bar{G}$ }\}
,
H
=
\emptyset

3:

repeat

4:

Add to
H
those nodes that have at least one child in
L
.

5:

Add to
L
those nodes that have only children in
H
.

6:

until
No new nodes are added to either set

Lemma D.11

(Lemma
3
in
Karp and Sipser [1981]
)

Let
\bar{G}
be a random tree rooted at
v.

(i)

v
is a target iff
v
is in
H
;

(ii)

v
is a loser iff either
v
is in
L
or
v
has exactly
1
child which is not in
H
.

Given that the nodes across different tree layers exhibit heterogeneity, we define two sets of nodes: the set
S_{l}
contains all nodes sampled from layers with
\text{Bernoulli}(b_{l})
and the set
S_{r}
contains all nodes from layers with
\text{Bernoulli}(b_{r})
. For a flexible node in
S_{l}
, we denote the probabilities of it being in
L
and
H
as
y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})
and
y_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})
, respectively. Similarly, for a regular node in
S_{l}
, we denote the probabilities as
y_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})
and
y_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})
.
We use an additional hat symbol to denote the counterparts of these probabilities in
S_{r}
, a notation consistently applied throughout this paper to differentiate quantities associated with
S_{r}
from those associated with
S_{l}
. In
Lemma

D.12
, we state that the vector

\mathbf{y}=\left(y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b}),y_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b}),y_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b}),y_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b}),\hat{y}_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b}),\hat{y}_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b}),\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b}),\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)

can be computed as the smallest set of solutions to the equations in (
14
).

Lemma D.12

As
n\rightarrow\infty
the probabilities encoded in
\mathbf{y}
converge to the smallest solution to (
14
).

Now, combining
Lemma

D.11
with
Lemma

D.12
, we obtain the probability for nodes in
\bar{G}
to be targets or losers.

Lemma D.13

Let
\bar{G}
be a random tree rooted at
v.
Then, as
n\rightarrow\infty
,

(i)

if
v
is a flexible node in
S_{l}
,
v
is a target with probability
y_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})
and a loser with probability

y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\left((1+b_{r})\alpha^{f}+(1-b_{r})\alpha-b_{r}2\alpha^{f}\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})-\left(1-b_{r}\right)\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right).

(ii)

if
v
is a regular node in
S_{l}
,
v
is a target with probability
y_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})
and a loser with probability

y_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})+y_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\left(b_{r}\alpha^{f}+(2-b_{r})\alpha-b_{r}\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})-\left(1-b_{r}\right)2\alpha\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right).

(iii)

if
v
is a flexible node in
S_{r}
,
v
is a target with probability
\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})
and a loser with probability

\hat{y}_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+\hat{y}_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\left((1+b_{l})\alpha^{f}+(1-b_{l})\alpha-b_{l}2\alpha^{f}y_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})-\left(1-b_{l}\right)\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)y_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right).

(iv)

if
v
is a regular node in
S_{r}
,
v
is a target with probability
\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})
and a loser with probability

\hat{y}_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})+\hat{y}_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\left(b_{l}\alpha^{f}+(2-b_{l})\alpha-b_{l}\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)y_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})-\left(1-b_{l}\right)2\alpha y_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right).

Equipped with
Lemma

D.13
, we are ready to compute the probability that
v
appears in a derivation as
n\rightarrow\infty.

Proposition D.14

(Extension of Theorem
9
(4) in
Karp and Sipser [1981]
)

Let
v
be a random node in
G
. Then, as
n\rightarrow\infty
:

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[v\text{ in a derivation}\mid v\text{ is a flexible node in }V_{l}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle y_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\left[(1+b_{r})\alpha^{f}+(1-b_{r})\alpha\right]\cdot

\displaystyle\left[1-\frac{b_{r}2\alpha^{f}}{(1+b_{r})\alpha^{f}+(1-b_{r})\alpha}\left(\hat{y}_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\right)-\frac{(1-b_{r})\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)}{(1+b_{r})\alpha^{f}+(1-b_{r})\alpha}\left(\hat{y}_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})+\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)\right],

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[v\text{ in a derivation}\mid v\text{ is a regular node in }V_{l}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle y_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})+y_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})+y_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\left[b_{r}\alpha^{f}+(2-b_{r})\alpha\right]\cdot

\displaystyle\left[1-\frac{b_{r}\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)}{b_{r}\alpha^{f}+(2-b_{r})\alpha}\left(\hat{y}_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\right)-\frac{(1-b_{r})2\alpha}{b_{r}\alpha^{f}+(2-b_{r})\alpha}\left(\hat{y}_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})+\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)\right],

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[v\text{ in a derivation}\mid v\text{ is a flexible node in }V_{r}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+\hat{y}_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+\hat{y}_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\left[(1+b_{l})\alpha^{f}+(1-b_{l})\alpha\right]\cdot

\displaystyle\left[1-\frac{b_{l}2\alpha^{f}}{(1+b_{l})\alpha^{f}+(1-b_{l})\alpha}\left(y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+y_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\right)-\frac{(1-b_{l})\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)}{(1+b_{l})\alpha^{f}+(1-b_{l})\alpha}\left(y_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})+y_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)\right],

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[v\text{ in a derivation}\mid v\text{ is a regular node in }V_{r}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})+\hat{y}_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})+\hat{y}_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\left[b_{l}\alpha^{f}+(2-b_{l})\alpha\right]\cdot

\displaystyle\left[1-\frac{b_{l}\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)}{b_{l}\alpha^{f}+(2-b_{l})\alpha}\left(y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+y_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\right)-\frac{(1-b_{l})2\alpha}{b_{l}\alpha^{f}+(2-b_{l})\alpha}\left(y_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})+y_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)\right].

Now, equipped with the auxiliary results, we are ready to prove
Theorem

D.3
,
Lemma

D.4
and
Theorem

D.5
.

Proof of
Theorem

D.3
Recall that the KS algorithm is asymptotically optimal under
Condition

1
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mu(b_{l},b_{r})

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{M}_{n}(b_{l},b_{r})\right]}{n}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[|M_{1}^{l}|+|M_{2}^{l}|\right]}{n}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[n-\psi_{1}-\psi_{2}\right]}{n}

\displaystyle=1-\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\psi_{1}\right]}{n}-\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\psi_{2}\right]}{n}=1-\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\psi_{1}\right]}{n}

provided that these limits exist. From
Proposition

D.10
(v), we know

\displaystyle\mu(b_{l},b_{r})=1-\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\psi_{1}\right]}{n}=

\displaystyle\;1-\lim_{n\to\infty}\max\left\{\mathbb{P}\left[v\in\Psi^{l}_{1}|v\in V_{l}\right],\mathbb{P}\left[v\in\Psi^{r}_{1}|v\in V_{r}\right]\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;1-\max\Bigl\{\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[v\text{ is a loser}\mid v\in V_{l}\right]-\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[v\text{ is a target}\mid v\in V_{r}\right],

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[v\text{ is a loser}\mid v\in V_{r}\right]-\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[v\text{ is a target}\mid v\in V_{l}\right]\Bigr\}

provided that these limits exist.
By the law of iterated expectations, provided that these limits exist, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[v\text{ is a loser}\mid v\in V_{l}\right]-\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[v\text{ is a target}\mid v\in V_{r}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[v\text{ is a loser}\mid v\text{ is a flexible node in }V_{l}\right]\cdot\mathbb{P}\left[v\in v_{l}\text{ is a flexible node}\right]

\displaystyle+\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[v\text{ is a loser}\mid v\text{ is a regular node in }V_{l}\right]\cdot\mathbb{P}\left[v\in v_{l}\text{ is a regular node}\right]

\displaystyle-\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[v\text{ is a target}\mid v\text{ is a flexible node in }V_{r}\right]\cdot\mathbb{P}\left[v\in v_{r}\text{ is a flexible node}\right]

\displaystyle-\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[v\text{ is a target}\mid v\text{ is a regular node in }V_{r}\right]\cdot\mathbb{P}\left[v\in v_{r}\text{ is a regular node}\right].

Now we can plug in probabilities derived in
Lemma

D.13
, which we have shown in
Claim

6
to be equal to the corresponding probabilities in random graphs:

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[v\text{ is a loser}\mid v\in V_{l}\right]-\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[v\text{ is a target}\mid v\in V_{r}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle b_{l}\left(y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\left((1+b_{r})\alpha^{f}+(1-b_{r})\alpha-b_{r}2\alpha^{f}\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})-\left(1-b_{r}\right)\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)\right)-b_{r}\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})

\displaystyle+(1-b_{l})\left(y_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})+y_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\left(b_{r}\alpha^{f}+(2-b_{r})\alpha-b_{r}\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})-\left(1-b_{r}\right)2\alpha\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)\right)-(1-b_{r})\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle b_{l}\left(y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\left(b_{r}2\alpha^{f}\left(1-\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\right)+\left(1-b_{r}\right)\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)\left(1-\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)\right)\right)-1+b_{r}\left(1-\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\right)

\displaystyle+(1-b_{l})\left(y_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})+y_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\left(b_{r}\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)\left(1-\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\right)+\left(1-b_{r}\right)2\alpha\left(1-\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)\right)\right)+(1-b_{r})\left(1-\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;b_{l}y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+b_{r}\left(1-\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\right)\left(2b_{l}\alpha^{f}y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+(1-b_{l})(\alpha^{f}+\alpha)y_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)-1+b_{r}\left(1-\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\right)

\displaystyle+(1-b_{l})y_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})+(1-b_{r})\left(1-\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)\left(b_{l}(\alpha^{f}+\alpha)y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+2(1-b_{l})\alpha y_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)+(1-b_{r})\left(1-\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}

\displaystyle 1-\xi(b_{l},b_{r}).

Similarly, we find that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[v\text{ is a loser}\mid v\in V_{r}\right]-\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[v\text{ is a target}\mid v\in V_{l}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;b_{r}\hat{y}_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+b_{l}\left(1-y_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\right)\left(2b_{r}\alpha^{f}\hat{y}_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+(1-b_{r})(\alpha^{f}+\alpha)\hat{y}_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)-1+b_{l}\left(1-y_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\right)

\displaystyle+(1-b_{r})\hat{y}_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})+(1-b_{l})\left(1-y_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)\left(b_{r}(\alpha^{f}+\alpha)\hat{y}_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+2(1-b_{r})\alpha\hat{y}_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)+(1-b_{l})\left(1-y_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}

\displaystyle 1-\hat{\xi}(b_{l},b_{r}).

Thus, under
Condition

1

\displaystyle\mu(b_{l},b_{r})=

\displaystyle\;1-\max\Bigl\{\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[v\text{ is a loser}\mid v\in V_{l}\right]-\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[v\text{ is a target}\mid v\in V_{r}\right],

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[v\text{ is a loser}\mid v\in V_{r}\right]-\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[v\text{ is a target}\mid v\in V_{l}\right]\Bigr\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;1-\max\left(1-\xi(b_{l},b_{r}),1-\hat{\xi}(b_{l},b_{r})\right)=\min\left(\xi(b_{l},b_{r}),\hat{\xi}(b_{l},b_{r})\right)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mu^{\texttt{KS}}(b_{l},b_{r}).\hfill\square

Proof of
Lemma

D.4
.

From
Proposition

D.10
(i), it is known that

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[m_{1}+\psi_{1}\right]}{n}=\max\left\{\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[v\text{ in a derivation}\mid v\in V_{l}\right],\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[v\text{ in a derivation}\mid v\in V_{r}\right]\right\}.

We next demonstrate that if the solution to (
14
) is unique, then

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[v\text{ in a derivation}\mid v\in V_{l}\right]=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[v\text{ in a derivation}\mid v\in V_{r}\right]=1.

(17)

To establish (
17
), we leverage
Proposition

D.14
and consider a random node
v
in
G
, which can be either flexible or regular and either in
V_{l}
or in
V_{r}
. be a flexible node in
V_{l}
, a regular node in
V_{l}
, a flexible node in
V_{r}
, or a regular node in
V_{r}
. We shall demonstrate that
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[v\text{ in a derivation}\right]=1
when the solution to (
14
) is unique.

Note that for any solution vector
\mathbf{w}
to (
14
), it is always feasible to construct

\begin{split}x_{L}^{f}(b_{l},b_{r})&=w_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b}),x_{H}^{f}(b_{l},b_{r})=1-w_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b}),\\
x_{L}^{nf}(b_{l},b_{r})&=w_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b}),x_{H}^{nf}(b_{l},b_{r})=1-w_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b}),\\
\hat{x}_{L}^{f}(b_{l},b_{r})&=\hat{w}_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b}),\hat{x}_{H}^{f}(b_{l},b_{r})=1-\hat{w}_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b}),\\
\hat{x}_{L}^{nf}(b_{l},b_{r})&=\hat{w}_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b}),\hat{x}_{H}^{nf}(b_{l},b_{r})=1-\hat{w}_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b}),\\
\end{split}

(18)

so that
\mathbf{x}
is provably a solution to (
14
). Consequently, when (
14
) admits a unique solution, the smallest set of solutions
\mathbf{y}
must satisfy (
18
). We next substitute (
18
) into the expressions derived in
Proposition

D.14
and simplify to find that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[v\text{ in a derivation}\mid v\text{ is a flexible node in }V_{l}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle y_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\left[(1+b_{r})\alpha^{f}+(1-b_{r})\alpha\right]\cdot

\displaystyle\left[1-\frac{b_{r}2\alpha^{f}}{(1+b_{r})\alpha^{f}+(1-b_{r})\alpha}\left(\hat{y}_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\right)-\frac{(1-b_{r})\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)}{(1+b_{r})\alpha^{f}+(1-b_{r})\alpha}\left(\hat{y}_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})+\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;1+y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\left[(1+b_{r})\alpha^{f}+(1-b_{r})\alpha\right]\cdot\left[1-\frac{b_{r}2\alpha^{f}}{(1+b_{r})\alpha^{f}+(1-b_{r})\alpha}-\frac{(1-b_{r})\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)}{(1+b_{r})\alpha^{f}+(1-b_{r})\alpha}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;1+y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\left[(1+b_{r})\alpha^{f}+(1-b_{r})\alpha\right]\cdot 0\quad=1.

One can show analogously that nodes in
V_{r}
or regular nodes in
V_{l}
have a probability of 1, asymptotically, to be in a derivation.
Consequently, we can verify
Condition

1
via

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\psi_{2}\right]}{n}\leq 1-\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[m_{1}+\psi_{1}\right]}{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;1-\max\left\{\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[v\text{ in a derivation}\mid v\in V_{l}\right],\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[v\text{ in a derivation}\mid v\in V_{r}\right]\right\}=0.\square

Proof D.15

Proof of
Theorem

D.5

By
Theorem

D.3
and
Lemma

D.4
, to establish
Theorem

D.5
it is sufficient to demonstrate that when
10^{-4}<\alpha<\alpha^{f}
and
\alpha^{f}+\alpha<e
the solution to (
14
) is unique at
\mathbf{b}\in\{(1,0),(0,1),(1/2,1/2)\}
.

When
\mathbf{b}=(1,0)
, all nodes in
V_{l}
are flexible nodes, while those in
V_{r}
are regular nodes. Thus, it suffices to analyze
w_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b}),w_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b}),\hat{w}_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})
and
\hat{w}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})
. Then, (
14
) reduces to

\displaystyle w_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})=e^{-\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)\left(1-\hat{w}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)},\hat{w}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})=1-e^{-\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)w_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})},

(19)

\displaystyle\hat{w}_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})=e^{-\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)\left(1-w_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\right)},w_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})=1-e^{-\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)\hat{w}_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})}.

(20)

Since (
19
) and (
20
) are equivalent, it suffices to show that solution to the pair
\left(w_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b}),\hat{w}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)
in (
19
) is unique. This is a direct application of the following result from
Karp and Sipser [1981]
, by taking
L=w_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b}),W=\hat{w}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})
and
\lambda=\alpha^{f}+\alpha\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}

Claim 1

(Lemma
1
in
Karp and Sipser [1981]
)

Define
L=e^{-\lambda(1-W)},W=1-e^{-\lambda L}.
Then,
L+W\leq 1,
with equality if and only if
\lambda\leq e.

The case for
\mathbf{b}=(0,1)
is symmetric.

For the case of
\mathbf{b}=(1/2,1/2)
, symmetry implies that

w_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})=\hat{w}_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b}),w_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})=\hat{w}_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b}),w_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})=\hat{w}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b}),w_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})=\hat{w}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b}).

Substituting
\mathbf{b}=(1/2,1/2)
into (
14
), we obtain the following equations:

\begin{split}w_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})=&\;e^{-\frac{1}{2}2\alpha^{f}\left(1-\hat{w}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\right)-\frac{1}{2}\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)\left(1-\hat{w}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)},\\
w_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})=&\;e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)\left(1-\hat{w}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\right)-\frac{1}{2}2\alpha\left(1-\hat{w}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)},\\
\hat{w}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})=&\;1-e^{-\frac{1}{2}2\alpha^{f}w_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})-\frac{1}{2}\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)w_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})},\\
\hat{w}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})=&\;1-e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)w_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})-\frac{1}{2}2\alpha w_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})}.\\
\end{split}

(21)

We observe that values of
w_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b}),w_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b}),\hat{w}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})
and
\hat{w}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})
are trivially bounded between
0
and
1,
and all of the variables are increasing in each other. Thus, we initialize the values of
\left(w_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b},0),w_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b},0),\hat{w}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b},0),\hat{w}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b},0)\right)=(0,0,0,0)
and define, for any
d\in\mathbb{Z}^{+},

\displaystyle w_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b},d)=

\displaystyle\;e^{-\frac{1}{2}2\alpha^{f}\left(1-\hat{w}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b},d-1)\right)-\frac{1}{2}\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)\left(1-\hat{w}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b},d-1)\right)},

\displaystyle w_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b},d)=

\displaystyle\;e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)\left(1-\hat{w}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b},d-1)\right)-\frac{1}{2}2\alpha\left(1-\hat{w}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b},d-1)\right)},

\displaystyle\hat{w}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b},d)=

\displaystyle\;1-e^{-\frac{1}{2}2\alpha^{f}w_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b},d-1)-\frac{1}{2}\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)w_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b},d-1)},

\displaystyle\hat{w}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b},d)=

\displaystyle\;1-e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)w_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b},d-1)-\frac{1}{2}2\alpha w_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b},d-1)}.

Then, the smallest set of solutions to (
21
) is given by
\lim_{d\to\infty}\left(w_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b},d),w_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b},d),\hat{w}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b},d),\hat{w}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b},d)\right)
.

For any
\vec{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2},
define

\displaystyle F(\vec{\mathbf{x}})=\begin{pmatrix}e^{-\frac{1}{2}2\alpha^{f}x_{1}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)x_{2}}\\
e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)x_{1}-\frac{1}{2}2\alpha x_{2}}\end{pmatrix}

and define
F^{t}(\vec{\mathbf{x}})
the
t
th application of the function
F
on
\vec{\mathbf{x}}.
That is,
F^{0}(\vec{\mathbf{x}})=\vec{\mathbf{x}},F^{1}(\vec{\mathbf{x}})=F(\vec{\mathbf{x}})
and
F^{2}(\vec{\mathbf{x}})=F\left(F(\vec{\mathbf{x}})\right).
Then, the smallest solutions to
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}w_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\\
w_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\end{smallmatrix}\right)
and
\vec{1}-\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\hat{w}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\\
\hat{w}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\end{smallmatrix}\right)
are respectively given by
\lim_{t\to\infty}F^{2t+1}(\vec{1})
and
\lim_{t\to\infty}F^{2t}(\vec{1})
. In particular,
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}w_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\\
w_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\vec{1}-\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\hat{w}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\\
\hat{w}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\end{smallmatrix}\right)
and the solution is unique if
F(\vec{\mathbf{x}})
has a unique fixed point, i.e., there exists a unique
\vec{\mathbf{x}^{\star}}
such that
F\left(\vec{\mathbf{x}^{\star}}\right)=\vec{\mathbf{x}^{\star}}.
Notice that in
F(\vec{\mathbf{x}})=\vec{\mathbf{x}}
we have
x_{1}=e^{-\alpha^{f}x_{1}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)x_{2}},
so
x_{2}=-2\frac{\log(x_{1})+\alpha^{f}x_{1}}{\alpha^{f}+\alpha}.
Plugging this into
x_{2}=e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)x_{1}-\frac{1}{2}2\alpha x_{2}},
we find that

-2\frac{\log(x_{1})+\alpha^{f}x_{1}}{\alpha^{f}+\alpha}=e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)x_{1}+2\frac{\alpha}{\alpha^{f}+\alpha}\left(\log(x_{1})+\alpha^{f}x_{1}\right)},

(22)

so it suffices to show that (
22
) has a unique solution when
\alpha^{f}+\alpha<e.
Let

f_{1}(x_{1})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)x_{1}+2\frac{\alpha}{\alpha^{f}+\alpha}\left(\log(x_{1})+\alpha^{f}x_{1}\right)}+2\frac{\log(x_{1})+\alpha^{f}x_{1}}{\alpha^{f}+\alpha}.

(23)

The next result establishes a monotonicity property of this function:

Claim 2

When
10^{-4}<\alpha<\alpha^{f}
and
\alpha^{f}+\alpha<e
,
f_{1}^{\prime}(x_{1})>1
for any
x_{1}\in(0,1]
.

Since
f_{1}(0)=-\infty
and
f_{1}(1)\geq 0,
by continuity of
f_{1}(x_{1})
with respect to
x_{1}
we know that
f_{1}(x_{1})=0
has at least one solution in
(0,1].
Since we also know from
Claim

2
that
f_{1}(x_{1})
is strictly monotonically increasing with respect to
x_{1}
in
(0,1]
when
10^{-4}<\alpha<\alpha^{f}
and
\alpha^{f}+\alpha<e
, such solution for
x_{1}
is unique. This completes the proof of
Theorem

D.5
.

D.5.3
Proof of
Theorem

D.6

Recall from
Theorem

D.5
that, in the stated parameter regimes,
\mu(b_{l},b_{r})=\mu^{\texttt{KS}}(b_{l},b_{r})
at
\mathbf{b}=(1,0)
and
(1/2,1/2)
.
Since the solution to (
14
) is unique at these points, (
18
) is satisfied by the smallest set of solutions
\mathbf{y}
. Plugging

y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})=1-y_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b}),y_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})=1-y_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})

into (
15
) and (
16
), we find that at these values of
\mathbf{b}

\begin{split}\mu(b_{l},b_{r})=&\;\xi(b_{l},b_{r})=\hat{\xi}(b_{l},b_{r})\\
=&\;2-b_{l}y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})-b_{r}e^{-b_{1}(b_{l},b_{r})}\left(1+b_{1}(b_{l},b_{r})\right)-(1-b_{l})y_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})-(1-b_{r})e^{-b_{2}(b_{l},b_{r})}\left(1+b_{2}(b_{l},b_{r})\right),\end{split}

\displaystyle\text{where}\qquad b_{1}(b_{l},b_{r})=

\displaystyle\;b_{l}2\alpha^{f}y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+(1-b_{l})\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)y_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b}),

\displaystyle b_{2}(b_{l},b_{r})=

\displaystyle\;b_{l}\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+(1-b_{l})2\alpha y_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b}).

When
\mathbf{b}=(1,0),

\mu(b_{l},b_{r})
depends only on
y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})
, which can be solved as the unique solution
x^{\star}
to

\displaystyle x=e^{-\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)e^{-\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)x}}.

(24)

When
\mathbf{b}=(1/2,1/2),

\mu(b_{l},b_{r})
depends only on
\left(y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b}),y_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)
, which can be solved as the unique set of solution
\left(x^{\star}_{1},x^{\star}_{2}\right)
to

\begin{split}x_{1}=&\;e^{-\frac{1}{2}2\alpha^{f}x_{1}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)x_{2}},\\
x_{2}=&e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)x_{1}-\frac{1}{2}2\alpha x_{2}}.\end{split}

(25)

Our objective is to show that
\mu(1,0)>\mu(1/2,1/2)
for any
\alpha^{f}
and
\alpha
satisfying
10^{-4}<\alpha<\alpha^{f}
and
\alpha^{f}+\alpha<e
.
To do so, we divide the parameter regions into small cells and prove the inequality by deriving bounds within each cell. We fix a constant
\delta>0
and derive for any
\alpha,\alpha^{f},
a lower bound for the expression
\mu(1,0)-\mu(1/2,1/2)
for any
(\bar{\alpha}^{f},\bar{\alpha})\in\mathcal{I}(\alpha,\alpha^{f},\delta)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\left\{(\bar{\alpha},\bar{\alpha^{f}})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\bar{\alpha}\in[\alpha,\alpha+\delta),\bar{\alpha^{f}}\in[\alpha^{f},\alpha^{f}+\delta)\right\}
. We then employ a computer-aided proof to iterate over all cells in the claimed region and verify if this lower bound is positive for the respective cell. To do so, we need to bound
\bar{\alpha}^{f},\bar{\alpha}
and the resulting
\bar{x},\bar{x}_{1}
and
\bar{x}_{2}
in each cell. In
\mathcal{I}(\alpha,\alpha^{f},\delta)
, if we know the respective lower and upper bounds of
\bar{x},\bar{x}_{1}
and
\bar{x}_{2}
, which we denote by
x^{lb},x^{ub},x^{lb}_{1},x^{ub}_{1},x^{lb}_{2}
and
x^{ub}_{2}
, then we can lower bound

\begin{split}\mu(1,0)=&\;2-\bar{x}-e^{-\left(\bar{\alpha}^{f}+\bar{\alpha}\right)\bar{x}}\left[1+\left(\bar{\alpha}^{f}+\bar{\alpha}\right)\bar{x}\right]\\
\geq&\;2-x^{ub}-e^{-\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)x^{lb}}\left[1+\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha+2\delta\right)x^{ub}\right],\forall\left(\bar{\alpha}^{f},\bar{\alpha}\right)\in\mathcal{I}(\alpha,\alpha^{f},\delta).\end{split}

(26)

Similarly, we can upper bound

\begin{split}\mu(1/2,1/2)=&\;2-\frac{1}{2}\bar{x}_{1}-\frac{1}{2}\bar{x}_{2}-\frac{1}{2}e^{-\left(\bar{\alpha}^{f}\right)\bar{x}_{1}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\bar{\alpha}^{f}+\bar{\alpha}\right)\bar{x}_{2}}\left[1+\bar{\alpha}^{f}\bar{x}_{1}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\bar{\alpha}^{f}+\bar{\alpha}\right)\bar{x}_{2}\right]\\
&-\frac{1}{2}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(\bar{\alpha}^{f}+\bar{\alpha}\right)\bar{x}_{1}-\left(\bar{\alpha}\right)\bar{x}_{2}}\left[1+\frac{1}{2}\left(\bar{\alpha}^{f}+\bar{\alpha}\right)\bar{x}_{1}+\bar{\alpha}\bar{x}_{2}\right]\\
\leq&\;2-\frac{1}{2}x^{lb}_{1}-\frac{1}{2}x^{lb}_{2}\\
&-\frac{1}{2}e^{-\left(\alpha^{f}+\delta\right)x^{ub}_{1}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha+2\delta\right)x^{ub}_{2}}\left[1+\alpha^{f}x^{lb}_{1}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)x^{lb}_{2}\right]\\
&-\frac{1}{2}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha+2\delta\right)x^{ub}_{1}-\left(\alpha+\delta\right)x^{ub}_{2}}\left[1+\frac{1}{2}\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)x^{lb}_{1}+\alpha x^{lb}_{2}\right],\forall\left(\bar{\alpha}^{f},\bar{\alpha}\right)\in\mathcal{I}(\alpha,\alpha^{f},\delta).\end{split}

(27)

Thus, it suffices to find
x^{lb},x^{ub},x^{lb}_{1},x^{ub}_{1},x^{lb}_{2}
and
x^{ub}_{2}
in the corresponding cell. To do this, we start by showing that, for given
\alpha^{f}
and
\alpha,
the solution returned by
nlsolve
package in Julia programming language is provably close to the true solution
x^{\star},x^{\star}_{1}
and
x^{\star}_{2}
, and then provide a continuity argument to bound the solution
\bar{x},\bar{x}_{1}
and
\bar{x}_{2}
for any
\left(\bar{\alpha}^{f},\bar{\alpha}\right)\in\mathcal{I}(\alpha,\alpha^{f},\delta)
.

We start by bounding the value of
x^{\star},x^{\star}_{1}
, and
x^{\star}_{2}
based on the numerical solutions returned by Julia
nlsolve
. We parameterize the tolerance level
ftol
in
nlsolve
by
\epsilon
, which guarantees that, when solving the equation
g(x)=0
for any function
g\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
,
nlsolve
returns a solution
x
such that
|g(x)|<\epsilon
. We let
f(x)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=x-e^{-\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)x}
and recall from (
23
) that
f_{1}(x_{1})=e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)x_{1}+2\frac{\alpha}{\alpha^{f}+\alpha}\left(\log(x_{1})+\alpha^{f}x_{1}\right)}+2\frac{\log(x_{1})+\alpha^{f}x_{1}}{\alpha^{f}+\alpha}.
Then, we leverage monotonicity properties of
f(x)
and
f_{1}(x_{1})
to derive the following bounds on
x^{\star},x^{\star}_{1}
and
x^{\star}_{2}
:

Claim 3

Let
x^{sol}
and
x^{sol}_{1}
respectively denote the solutions returned by
nlsolve
for solving
f(x)=0
and
f_{1}(x_{1})=0
with
ftol=\epsilon.
When
10^{-4}<\alpha<\alpha^{f}
and
\alpha^{f}+\alpha<e
, we have:

\displaystyle x^{\star}\in\left[x^{sol}-\epsilon,x^{sol}+\epsilon\right],x^{\star}_{1}\in\left[x^{sol}_{1}-\epsilon,x^{sol}_{1}+\epsilon\right],\text{ and}

\displaystyle x^{\star}_{2}\in\left[-2\frac{\log(x^{sol}_{1}+\epsilon)+\alpha^{f}\left(x^{sol}_{1}+\epsilon\right)}{\alpha^{f}+\alpha},-2\frac{\log(x^{sol}_{1}-\epsilon)+\alpha^{f}\left(x^{sol}_{1}-\epsilon\right)}{\alpha^{f}+\alpha}\right].

Having established bounds on
x^{\star},x^{\star}_{1}
and
x^{\star}_{2}
for fixed
\alpha
and
\alpha^{f}
, we continue to bound
\bar{x},\bar{x}_{1}
and
\bar{x}_{2}
for
\left(\bar{\alpha}^{f},\bar{\alpha}\right)\in\mathcal{I}(\alpha,\alpha^{f},\delta)
. Specifically, we leverage the following result on the continuity of
x^{\star},x^{\star}_{1}
and
x^{\star}_{2}
with respect to
\alpha^{f}
and
\alpha.

Claim 4

Let
x^{\star},x^{\star}_{1}
and
x^{\star}_{2}
be the solution to (
24
) and (
25
) given
\alpha^{f}
and
\alpha.
Moreover, let
\bar{x},\bar{x}_{1}
and
\bar{x}_{2}
be the solution to (
24
) and (
25
) given
\bar{\alpha}^{f}
and
\bar{\alpha}.
Then, given any
\delta\in(0,1/2)
, we know that for any
\left(\bar{\alpha}^{f},\bar{\alpha}\right)\in\mathcal{I}(\alpha,\alpha^{f},\delta)
such that
10^{-4}<\bar{\alpha}<\bar{\alpha^{f}}
and
\bar{\alpha}^{f}+\bar{\alpha}<e
:

(i)

\bar{x}\in\left[x^{\star}(1-2\delta),x^{\star}\right];

(ii)

\bar{x}_{1}\in\left[x^{\star}_{1}(1-2\delta),x^{\star}_{1}\right]
and
\bar{x}_{2}\in\left[x^{\star}_{2}(1-2\delta),x^{\star}_{2}\right].

Combining
Claim

3
and
Claim

4
, we can lower and upper bound the solution of
\bar{x},\bar{x}_{1}
and
\bar{x}_{2}
for
\left(\bar{\alpha}^{f},\bar{\alpha}\right)\in\mathcal{I}(\alpha,\alpha^{f},\delta)
where
\bar{\alpha^{f}}+\bar{\alpha}<e
. In particular, for given
\delta>0
, we take

\begin{split}x^{lb}&=\left(x^{sol}-\epsilon\right)\left(1-2\delta\right),x^{ub}=\left(x^{sol}+\epsilon\right),\\
x^{lb}_{1}&=\left(x^{sol}_{1}-\epsilon\right)\left(1-2\delta\right),x^{ub}_{1}=\left(x^{sol}_{1}+\epsilon\right),\\
x^{lb}_{2}&=-2\frac{\log(x^{sol}_{1}+\epsilon)+\alpha^{f}\left(x^{sol}_{1}+\epsilon\right)}{\alpha^{f}+\alpha}\left(1-2\delta\right),\\
x^{ub}_{2}&=-2\frac{\log(x^{sol}_{1}-\epsilon)+\alpha^{f}\left(x^{sol}_{1}-\epsilon\right)}{\alpha^{f}+\alpha}.\end{split}

(28)

Plugging these values into (
26
) - (
27
), we obtain a lower bound of
\mu(1,0)-\mu(1/2,1/2)
in each cell
\mathcal{I}(\alpha,\alpha^{f},\delta)
. Whenever this lower bound exceeds
0,
our reasoning implies that
\mu(1,0)>\mu(1/2,1/2)
for any
\left(\bar{\alpha}^{f},\bar{\alpha}\right)
within this cell. In
Theorem3.ipynb
,
11
11

11

The computer-aided proof can be found at
https://bit.ly/3UUVFRX
.
we fix
\epsilon=10^{-8}
and compute the value of this lower bound for
\alpha^{f},\alpha=\delta,2\delta,...,e
where
\alpha^{f}+\alpha<e
. We find that by taking
\delta=0.01,0.005,0.0025
and

we are able to verify
\mu(1,0)>\mu(1/2,1/2)
in the respective red regions in

.
\square

Proof D.16

Proof of
Claim

3

For
f(x)=x-e^{-\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)x},
we find that
f^{\prime}(x)>1\;\forall x
. When solving
f(x)=0,

nlsolve
is guaranteed to return a solution
x^{sol}
with
|f(x^{sol})|<\epsilon
. Since
f^{\prime}(x)>1\;\forall x
, if
x^{\star}>x^{sol}+\epsilon
,

\text{then}\qquad f(x^{\star})>f(x^{sol}+\epsilon)>f(x^{sol})+\epsilon>0,

contradicting the fact that
f(x^{\star})=0.
Similarly, we must have
x^{\star}>x^{sol}-\epsilon
and thus
x^{\star}\in[x^{sol}-\epsilon,x^{sol}+\epsilon].

Since we know from
Claim

2
that
f_{1}^{\prime}(x_{1})>1
in the specified parameter regime of
\alpha
and
\alpha^{f},
through the same argument on
f_{1}(x_{1})
we find that
x^{\star}_{1}\in[x^{sol}_{1}-\epsilon,x^{sol}_{1}+\epsilon].
From (
25
) we know that
x^{\star}_{2}=-2\frac{\log(x^{\star}_{1})+\alpha^{f}x^{\star}_{1}}{\alpha^{f}+\alpha},
which allows us to lower bound
x^{\star}_{2}
by
-2\frac{\log(x^{sol}_{1}+\epsilon)+\alpha^{f}\left(x^{sol}_{1}+\epsilon\right)}{\alpha^{f}+\alpha}
and upper bound
x_{2}
by
-2\frac{\log(x^{sol}_{1}-\epsilon)+\alpha^{f}\left(x^{sol}_{1}-\epsilon\right)}{\alpha^{f}+\alpha}.

Proof D.17

Proof of
Claim

4

We start by proving
Claim

4
(i). We have shown in the proof of
Theorem

D.5
that the solution to (
24
) is unique in the stated parameter regimes. Thus, we know that
x^{\star}=e^{-\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)x^{\star}}
because this construction trivially satisfies (
24
). Similarly,
\bar{x}=e^{-\left(\bar{\alpha}^{f}+\bar{\alpha}\right)\bar{x}}
. Now, given that
e^{-\left(\bar{\alpha}^{f}+\bar{\alpha}\right)x^{\star}}\leq e^{-\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)x^{\star}}=x^{\star}
for any
\left(\bar{\alpha}^{f},\bar{\alpha}\right)\in\mathcal{I}(\alpha,\alpha^{f},\delta)
, we know that
\bar{x}\leq x^{\star}.
Moreover, from the uniqueness of solution we know that it suffices to show that

x^{\star}(1-2\delta)\leq e^{-\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha+2\delta\right)x^{\star}(1-2\delta)}\leq e^{-\left(\bar{\alpha}^{f}+\bar{\alpha}\right)x^{\star}(1-2\delta)},

so then the unique solution
\bar{x}\geq x^{\star}\left(1-2\delta\right).
To do so, we observe that

e^{-\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha+2\delta\right)x^{\star}(1-2\delta)}\geq e^{-\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha+2\delta\right)x^{\star}}=e^{-\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)x^{\star}-2\delta x^{\star}}=x^{\star}e^{-2\delta x^{\star}},

\displaystyle\text{so it suffices to show that}\qquad x^{\star}(1-2\delta)\leq x^{\star}e^{-2\delta x^{\star}}.

(29)

As we trivially have
x^{\star}\in(0,1]
, we can cancel out
x^{\star}
and take logarithm on both sides.

\text{Thus, \eqref{eq:continuity1} is equivalent to}\quad-\log(1-2\delta)\geq 2\delta x^{\star},

\text{which is implied by}\quad-\log(1-2\delta)\geq 2\delta,\quad\text{which holds for all $\delta\in(0,1/2)$.}

We next prove
Claim

4
(ii). Given that in the claimed region

\displaystyle x^{\star}_{1}=

\displaystyle\;e^{-\frac{1}{2}2\alpha^{f}x^{\star}_{1}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)x^{\star}_{2}}\geq e^{-\frac{1}{2}2\bar{\alpha}^{f}x^{\star}_{1}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\bar{\alpha}^{f}+\bar{\alpha}\right)x^{\star}_{2}},

\displaystyle x^{\star}_{2}=

\displaystyle e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)x^{\star}_{1}-\frac{1}{2}2\alpha x^{\star}_{2}}\geq e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(\bar{\alpha}^{f}+\bar{\alpha}\right)x^{\star}_{1}-\frac{1}{2}2\bar{\alpha}x^{\star}_{2}}

for any
\left(\bar{\alpha}^{f},\bar{\alpha}\right)\in\mathcal{I}(\alpha,\alpha^{f},\delta)
, we know that
\bar{x}_{1}\leq x^{\star}_{1}
and
\bar{x}_{2}\leq x^{\star}_{2}.

To prove the claimed lower bound on
\bar{x}_{1}
and
\bar{x}_{2},
we show that

\displaystyle x^{\star}_{1}(1-2\delta)\leq

\displaystyle\;e^{-\frac{1}{2}2\bar{\alpha}^{f}x^{\star}_{1}(1-2\delta)-\frac{1}{2}\left(\bar{\alpha}^{f}+\bar{\alpha}\right)x^{\star}_{2}(1-2\delta)},

(30)

\displaystyle x^{\star}_{2}(1-2\delta)\leq

\displaystyle\;e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(\bar{\alpha}^{f}+\bar{\alpha}\right)x^{\star}_{1}(1-2\delta)-\frac{1}{2}2\bar{\alpha}x^{\star}_{2}(1-2\delta)}.

In particular, (
30
) is implied by

x^{\star}_{1}(1-2\delta)\leq e^{-\frac{1}{2}2\alpha^{f}x^{\star}_{1}-\delta x^{\star}_{1}(1-2\delta)-\frac{1}{2}\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)x^{\star}_{2}-\delta x^{\star}_{2}(1-2\delta)}=x^{\star}_{1}\cdot e^{-\delta x^{\star}_{1}(1-2\delta)-\delta x^{\star}_{2}(1-2\delta)}.

Since we trivially have
x_{1}^{\star}\in(0,1]
, we can cancel out
x_{1}^{\star}
and take logarithm on both sides. We find that (
30
) is equivalent to
-\log(1-2\delta)\geq(x^{\star}_{1}+x^{\star}_{2})\delta(1-2\delta).
Since
x^{\star}_{1}+x^{\star}_{2}\in[0,2],
it is then sufficient to show that
\frac{-\log(1-2\delta)}{2\delta(1-2\delta)}\geq 1,
which holds for all
\delta\in(0,1/2).
The proof of
\bar{x}_{2}\geq x^{\star}_{2}(1-2\delta)
is symmetric.

D.5.4
Proof of
Theorem

C.1

We start with the convexity result in
Theorem

C.1
(ii).
Recall from (
15
) that

\begin{split}\xi(b_{l},b_{r})=2&-b_{l}y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})-b_{r}\left(1-\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\right)\\
&-b_{r}\left(1-\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\right)\left(2b_{l}\alpha^{f}y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+(1-b_{l})(\alpha^{f}+\alpha)y_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)\\
&-(1-b_{l})y_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})-(1-b_{r})\left(1-\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)\\
&-(1-b_{r})\left(1-\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)\left(b_{l}(\alpha^{f}+\alpha)y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+2(1-b_{l})\alpha y_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right),\end{split}

Since we are interested in the direction
(1,-1),
for ease of notation we denote the sum of flexibility by
B
and replace
b_{r}
with
B-b_{l}
. Then, we can re-write
\xi(b_{l},b_{r})=\xi(b_{l},B-b_{l})
as

\begin{split}&2-b_{l}y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})-(B-b_{l})\left(1-\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\right)\\
&-(B-b_{l})\left(1-\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\right)\left(2b_{l}\alpha^{f}y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+(1-b_{l})(\alpha^{f}+\alpha)y_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)\\
&-(1-b_{l})y_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})-(1-B+b_{l})\left(1-\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)\\
&-(1-B+b_{l})\left(1-\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)\left(b_{l}(\alpha^{f}+\alpha)y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+2(1-b_{l})\alpha y_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right).\end{split}

(31)

Then the second-order derivative of
\xi(b_{l},b_{r})
in the direction
(1,-1)
equals
\frac{\partial^{2}\xi(b_{l},B-b_{l})}{\partial b_{l}^{2}}.
We observe that

\xi(b_{l},B-b_{l})=2-b_{l}y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})-(B-b_{l})e^{-b_{1}(b_{l})}(1+b_{1}(b_{l}))-(1-b_{l})y_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})-(1-B+b_{l})e^{-b_{2}(b_{l})}(1+b_{2}(b_{l})),

\displaystyle\text{where}\qquad b_{1}(b_{l})

\displaystyle=b_{l}\cdot 2\alpha^{f}\cdot y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+(1-b_{l})(\alpha^{f}+\alpha)y_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b}),

\displaystyle b_{2}(b_{l})

\displaystyle=b_{l}(\alpha^{f}+\alpha)y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+(1-b_{l})\cdot 2\alpha\cdot y_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b}).

\text{Let}\qquad x_{1}(b_{l},b_{r})=e^{-b_{l}2\alpha^{f}y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})-(1-b_{l})\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)y_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})}\text{ and }x_{2}(b_{l},b_{r})=e^{-b_{l}\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})-(1-b_{l})2\alpha y_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})}

(32)

For ease of notation we drop the dependency of
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
on
\mathbf{b}
. Then we simplify
\xi(b_{l},B-b_{l})
into

\displaystyle\xi(b_{l},B-b_{l})=2

\displaystyle-b_{l}e^{-(B-b_{l})2\alpha^{f}x_{1}-(1-B+b_{l})\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)x_{2}}-(B-b_{l})x_{1}(1-\log(x_{1}))

\displaystyle-(1-b_{l})e^{-(B-b_{l})\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)x_{1}-(1-B+b_{l})2\alpha x_{2}}-(1-B+b_{l})x_{2}(1-\log(x_{2})).

By construction of
x_{1},x_{2}
and the definition of
y_{L}^{f}(q),y_{L}^{nf}(q),
we have

\displaystyle x_{1}

\displaystyle=e^{-b_{l}2\alpha^{f}e^{-(B-b_{l})2\alpha^{f}x_{1}-(1-B+b_{l})(\alpha^{f}+\alpha)x_{2}}-(1-b_{l})\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)e^{-(B-b_{l})(\alpha^{f}+\alpha)x_{1}-(1-B+b_{l})2\alpha x_{2}}}

\displaystyle x_{2}

\displaystyle=e^{-b_{l}\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)e^{-(B-b_{l})2\alpha^{f}x_{1}-(1-B+b_{l})(\alpha^{f}+\alpha)x_{2}}-(1-b_{l})2\alpha e^{-(B-b_{l})(\alpha^{f}+\alpha)x_{1}-(1-B+b_{l})2\alpha x_{2}}}.

For convenience we write

y_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=e^{-(B-b_{l})2\alpha^{f}x_{1}-(1-B+b_{l})(\alpha^{f}+\alpha)x_{2}}\text{ and }y_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=e^{-(B-b_{l})(\alpha^{f}+\alpha)x_{1}-(1-B+b_{l})2\alpha x_{2}},

(33)

\text{so that }\qquad x_{1}=e^{-b_{l}2\alpha^{f}y_{1}-(1-b_{l})\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)y_{2}},x_{2}=e^{-b_{l}\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)y_{1}-(1-b_{l})2\alpha y_{2}}.

Now, taking second order derivative of
\xi(b_{l},B-b_{l})
with respect to
b_{l},
we obtain

\begin{split}\frac{\partial^{2}\xi(b_{l},B-b_{l})}{\partial b_{l}^{2}}=&\;-b_{l}\left(-2\alpha^{f}x_{1}+\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)x_{2}+(B-b_{l})2\alpha^{f}\frac{\partial x_{1}}{\partial b_{l}}+(1-B+b_{l})\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)\frac{\partial x_{2}}{\partial b_{l}}\right)^{2}y_{1}\\
&-(1-b_{l})\left(-\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)x_{1}+2\alpha x_{2}+(B-b_{l})\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)\frac{\partial x_{1}}{\partial b_{l}}+(1-B+b_{l})2\alpha\frac{\partial x_{2}}{\partial b_{l}}\right)^{2}y_{2}\\
&+2\left(-2\alpha^{f}y_{1}+\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)y_{2}\right)x_{1}+2\left(\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)y_{1}-2\alpha y_{2}\right)x_{2}\\
&+(B-b_{l})2\left(2\alpha^{f}y_{1}-\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)y_{2}\right)\left(\frac{\partial x_{1}}{\partial b_{l}}\right)+(1-B+b_{l})2\left(\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)y_{1}-2\alpha y_{2}\right)\left(\frac{\partial x_{2}}{\partial b_{l}}\right)\\
&+(B-b_{l})\left(\frac{\partial x_{1}}{\partial b_{l}}\right)^{2}/x_{1}+(1-B+b_{l})\left(\frac{\partial x_{2}}{\partial b_{l}}\right)^{2}/x_{2}.\end{split}
