Proof of
Claim

5

By definition, we need to show that

\nabla_{\mathbf{v}}^{2}\mu^{\texttt{KS}}(\mathbf{b}^{\prime})=\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{\mu^{\texttt{KS}}(\mathbf{b}^{\prime}+\mathbf{v}h)-2\mu^{\texttt{KS}}(\mathbf{b}^{\prime})+\mu^{\texttt{KS}}(\mathbf{b}^{\prime}-\mathbf{v}h)}{h^{2}}

exists and is equal to the claimed value. By Taylor series expansion, we know that for
h\in\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle\xi(\mathbf{b}^{\prime}+\mathbf{v}h)=

\displaystyle\;\xi(\mathbf{b}^{\prime})+h\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{v}}\xi(\mathbf{b}^{\prime})+h^{2}/2\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{v}}^{2}\xi(\mathbf{b}^{\prime})+o(h^{2}),

\displaystyle\hat{\xi}(\mathbf{b}^{\prime}+\mathbf{v}h)=

\displaystyle\;\hat{\xi}(\mathbf{b}^{\prime})+h\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{v}}\hat{\xi}(\mathbf{b}^{\prime})+h^{2}/2\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{v}}^{2}\hat{\xi}(\mathbf{b}^{\prime})+o(h^{2}),

\displaystyle\xi(\mathbf{b}^{\prime}-\mathbf{v}h)=

\displaystyle\;\xi(\mathbf{b}^{\prime})-h\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{v}}\xi(\mathbf{b}^{\prime})+h^{2}/2\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{v}}^{2}\xi(\mathbf{b}^{\prime})-o(h^{2}),

\displaystyle\hat{\xi}(\mathbf{b}^{\prime}-\mathbf{v}h)=

\displaystyle\;\hat{\xi}(\mathbf{b}^{\prime})-h\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{v}}\hat{\xi}(\mathbf{b}^{\prime})+h^{2}/2\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{v}}^{2}\hat{\xi}(\mathbf{b}^{\prime})-o(h^{2}).

In particular, as
\xi(\mathbf{b}^{\prime})=\hat{\xi}(\mathbf{b}^{\prime}),\nabla_{v}\xi(\mathbf{b}^{\prime})=\nabla_{v}\hat{\xi}(\mathbf{b}^{\prime})
and
\nabla_{v}^{2}\xi(\mathbf{b}^{\prime})=\nabla_{v}^{2}\hat{\xi}(\mathbf{b}^{\prime}),
from the above we know that

\xi(\mathbf{b}^{\prime}+\mathbf{v}h)-\hat{\xi}(\mathbf{b}^{\prime}+\mathbf{v}h)=o(h^{2})\text{ and }\xi(\mathbf{b}^{\prime}-\mathbf{v}h)-\hat{\xi}(\mathbf{b}^{\prime}-\mathbf{v}h)=o(h^{2}).

\displaystyle\text{ Thus,}\quad\nabla_{\mathbf{v}}^{2}\mu^{\texttt{KS}}(\mathbf{b}^{\prime})=

\displaystyle\;\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{\mu^{\texttt{KS}}(\mathbf{b}^{\prime}+\mathbf{v}h)-2\mu^{\texttt{KS}}(\mathbf{b}^{\prime})+\mu^{\texttt{KS}}(\mathbf{b}^{\prime}-\mathbf{v}h)}{h^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{\min\left(\xi(\mathbf{b}^{\prime}+\mathbf{v}h),\hat{\xi}(\mathbf{b}^{\prime}+\mathbf{v}h)\right)-2\min\left(\xi(\mathbf{b}^{\prime}),\hat{\xi}(\mathbf{b}^{\prime})\right)+\min\left(\xi(\mathbf{b}^{\prime}-\mathbf{v}h),\hat{\xi}(\mathbf{b}^{\prime}-\mathbf{v}h)\right)}{h^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{\xi(\mathbf{b}^{\prime}+\mathbf{v}h)-2\xi(\mathbf{b}^{\prime})+\xi(\mathbf{b}^{\prime}-\mathbf{v}h)+o(h^{2})}{h^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\nabla_{\mathbf{v}}^{2}\xi(\mathbf{b}^{\prime})=\nabla_{\mathbf{v}}^{2}\hat{\xi}(\mathbf{b}^{\prime}).\hfill\square

D.5.5
Proofs of the Auxiliary Results in Appendix
D.5.2

Proof D.18

Proof of
Proposition

D.10

Theorem
8
in
Karp and Sipser [1981]
(1) - (4) establishes the validity of the statements in
Proposition

D.10
(i) - (iv) for a general graph
G=(V,E)
. Since bipartite graphs are a subset of such general graphs, these results immediately extend.

For
Proposition

D.10
(v), to determine
|\Psi^{l}_{1}|
and
|\Psi^{r}_{1}|
, we start by finding
m_{1}
. By (iii), every edge in
M_{1}
is connected to at least one target. By (ii), if an edge in
M_{1}
is connected to two targets
u
and
v
, then
v\bigotimes u
and
u\bigotimes v
. Hence,

m_{1}=|M_{1}|\leq\left|\left\{v\in V_{l}\mid v\text{ is a target}\right\}\right|+\left|\left\{v\in V_{r}\mid v\text{ is a target}\right\}\right|-\left|\left\{(v,u)\mid v\bigotimes u\text{ and }u\bigotimes v\right\}\right|.

The equality follows from (ii) because every target is connected to an edge in
M_{1}
.

By Theorem 9 (4) in
Karp and Sipser [1981]
, a node
v
appears in a derivation if and only if it is a target or a loser or both. Furthermore,
v
is both a target and a loser if and only if there exists a unique
u
such that
v\bigotimes u
and
u\bigotimes v
. Hence, the number of nodes in
V_{l}
that appear in a derivation is given by

\left|\left\{v\in V_{l}\mid v\text{ is a target}\right\}\right|+\left|\left\{v\in V_{l}\mid v\text{ is a loser}\right\}\right|-\left|\left\{(v,u)\mid v\bigotimes u\text{ and }u\bigotimes v\right\}\right|.

Then, we find
\Psi^{l}_{1}
as the set of nodes in
V_{l}
that appear in a derivation but do not belong to
M^{l}_{1}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle|\Psi^{l}_{1}|=

\displaystyle\;\left|\left\{v\in V_{l}\mid v\text{ is a target}\right\}\right|+\left|\left\{v\in V_{l}\mid v\text{ is a loser}\right\}\right|-\left|\left\{(v,u)\mid v\bigotimes u\text{ and }u\bigotimes v\right\}\right|

\displaystyle-\left(\left|\left\{v\in V_{l}\mid v\text{ is a target}\right\}\right|+\left|\left\{v\in V_{r}\mid v\text{ is a target}\right\}\right|-\left|\left\{(v,u)\mid v\bigotimes u\text{ and }u\bigotimes v\right\}\right|\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\left|\left\{v\in V_{l}\mid v\text{ is a loser}\right\}\right|-\left|\left\{v\in V_{r}\mid v\text{ is a target}\right\}\right|.

Computing
|\Psi^{r}_{1}|
is symmetric. Since
\psi_{1}=\max\left\{|\Psi^{l}_{1}|,|\Psi^{r}_{1}|\right\}
by definition,
Proposition

D.10
(v) follows.

Proof D.19

Proof of
Lemma

D.12

We start from the leaf of a random tree, i.e.,
d=1,
and iteratively trace back to the root of the tree as
d
scales large. For a flexible node
u\in S_{l},
the number of its children follows a Binomial distribution
\text{Binom}\left(n,(1+b_{r})p^{f}_{n}+(1-b_{r})p_{n}\right)
. Thus, the probability for it to have
k
children is given by

z^{f}_{k}(\mathbf{b})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\binom{n}{k}\left((1+b_{r})p^{f}_{n}+(1-b_{r})p_{n}\right)^{k}\left(1-(1+b_{r})p^{f}_{n}-(1-b_{r})p_{n}\right)^{n-k}\;\forall k.

Moreover, since the probability that
u
connects with a flexible node is
2p^{f}
and the probability that it connects with a regular node is
p^{f}+p,
by Bayes’ Theorem we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[u^{\prime}\text{ is flexible}|u\text{ is flexible},u^{\prime}\text{ is a child of }u\right]=

\displaystyle\;\frac{b_{r}\cdot 2p^{f}_{n}}{b_{r}\cdot 2p^{f}_{n}+(1-b_{r})\cdot(p^{f}_{n}+p_{n})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\frac{b_{r}\cdot 2p^{f}_{n}}{(1+b_{r})p^{f}_{n}+(1-b_{r})p_{n}}

\text{and similarly}\qquad\mathbb{P}\left[u^{\prime}\text{ is regular}|u\text{ is flexible},u^{\prime}\text{ is a child of }u\right]=\frac{(1-b_{r})\cdot(p^{f}_{n}+p_{n})}{(1+b_{r})p^{f}_{n}+(1-b_{r})p_{n}}.

By definition,
u
is in
L
if all of its children are in
H
, including when it has no children. Thus,

\displaystyle y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b},d)=

\displaystyle\;\sum_{k=0}^{n}z^{f}_{k}(\mathbf{b})\cdot\left(\frac{2b_{r}p^{f}_{n}}{(1+b_{r})p^{f}_{n}+(1-b_{r})p_{n}}\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b},d-1)+\frac{(1-b_{r})\cdot(p^{f}_{n}+p_{n})}{(1+b_{r})p^{f}_{n}+(1-b_{r})p_{n}}\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b},d-1)\right)^{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}\left((1+b_{r})p^{f}_{n}+(1-b_{r})p_{n}\right)^{k}\left(1-(1+b_{r})p^{f}_{n}-(1-b_{r})p_{n}\right)^{n-k}

\displaystyle\cdot\left(\frac{2b_{r}p^{f}_{n}}{(1+b_{r})p^{f}_{n}+(1-b_{r})p_{n}}\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b},d-1)+\frac{(1-b_{r})\cdot(p^{f}_{n}+p_{n})}{(1+b_{r})p^{f}_{n}+(1-b_{r})p_{n}}\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b},d-1)\right)^{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}\left(1-(1+b_{r})p^{f}_{n}-(1-b_{r})p_{n}\right)^{n-k}\cdot\left(2b_{r}p^{f}_{n}\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b},d-1)+(1-b_{r})\cdot(p^{f}_{n}+p_{n})\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b},d-1)\right)^{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\left[2b_{r}p^{f}_{n}\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b},d-1)+(1-b_{r})\cdot(p^{f}_{n}+p_{n})\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b},d-1)+1-(1+b_{r})p^{f}_{n}-(1-b_{r})p_{n}\right]^{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\left[1-2b_{r}p^{f}_{n}\left(1-\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b},d-1)\right)-(1-b_{r})\cdot(p^{f}_{n}+p_{n})\left(1-\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b},d-1)\right)\right]^{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\left[1-\frac{2b_{r}\alpha^{f}\left(1-\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b},d-1)\right)+(1-b_{r})\cdot(\alpha^{f}+\alpha)\left(1-\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b},d-1)\right)}{n}\right]^{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;e^{-2b_{r}\alpha^{f}\left(1-\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b},d-1)\right)-(1-b_{r})\cdot(\alpha^{f}+\alpha)\left(1-\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b},d-1)\right)}\text{ as }n\rightarrow\infty.

Notice that the fourth equality is an application of the Binomial Theorem, and the last one follows from

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\left(1-\frac{x}{n}\right)^{n}=e^{-x},\forall x.

The expressions for
y_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b},d),\hat{y}_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b},d)
and
\hat{y}_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b},d)
can be derived in a similar fashion.

Next, by definition,
u
is in
H
if it has at least one child in
L
. Thus,

\displaystyle y_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b},d)=

\displaystyle\;1-\sum_{k=0}^{n}z^{f}_{k}(\mathbf{b})\cdot\left(\frac{2b_{r}p^{f}_{n}}{(1+b_{r})p^{f}_{n}+(1-b_{r})p_{n}}\left(1-\hat{y}_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b},d-1)\right)+\frac{(1-b_{r})\cdot(p^{f}_{n}+p_{n})}{(1+b_{r})p^{f}_{n}+(1-b_{r})p_{n}}\left(1-\hat{y}_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b},d-1)\right)\right)^{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;1-\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}\left((1+b_{r})p^{f}_{n}+(1-b_{r})p_{n}\right)^{k}\left(1-(1+b_{r})p^{f}_{n}-(1-b_{r})p_{n}\right)^{n-k}

\displaystyle\cdot\left(\frac{2b_{r}p^{f}_{n}}{(1+b_{r})p^{f}_{n}+(1-b_{r})p_{n}}\left(1-\hat{y}_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b},d-1)\right)+\frac{(1-b_{r})\cdot(p^{f}_{n}+p_{n})}{(1+b_{r})p^{f}_{n}+(1-b_{r})p_{n}}\left(1-\hat{y}_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b},d-1)\right)\right)^{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;1-\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}\left(1-(1+b_{r})p^{f}_{n}-(1-b_{r})p_{n}\right)^{n-k}

\displaystyle\cdot\left(2b_{r}p^{f}_{n}\left(1-\hat{y}_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b},d-1)\right)+(1-b_{r})\cdot(p^{f}_{n}+p_{n})\left(1-\hat{y}_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b},d-1)\right)\right)^{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;1-\left[2b_{r}p^{f}_{n}\left(1-\hat{y}_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b},d-1)\right)+(1-b_{r})\cdot(p^{f}_{n}+p_{n})\left(1-\hat{y}_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b},d-1)\right)+1-(1+b_{r})p^{f}_{n}-(1-b_{r})p_{n}\right]^{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;1-\left[1-2b_{r}p^{f}_{n}\hat{y}_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b},d-1)-(1-b_{r})\cdot(p^{f}_{n}+p_{n})\hat{y}_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b},d-1)\right]^{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;1-\left[1-\frac{2b_{r}\alpha^{f}\hat{y}_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b},d-1)+(1-b_{r})\cdot(\alpha^{f}+\alpha)\hat{y}_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b},d-1)}{n}\right]^{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;1-e^{-2b_{r}\alpha^{f}\hat{y}_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b},d-1)-(1-b_{r})\cdot(\alpha^{f}+\alpha)\hat{y}_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b},d-1)}\text{ as }n\rightarrow\infty.

The expressions for
y_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b},d),\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b},d)
and
\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b},d)
can be derived in a similar fashion. Since all leaf nodes are in
L
, we have

y_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b},1)=y_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b},1)=\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b},1)=\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b},1)=0.

Moreover, since
y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b},d),y_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b},d),y_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b},d),y_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b},d),\hat{y}_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b},d),\hat{y}_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b},d),\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b},d),\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b},d)
are all bounded increasing sequences with respect to
d,
these sequences converge as
d\rightarrow\infty
and
\mathbf{y}
is given by the smallest solution to (
14
).

Proof D.20

Proof of
Lemma

D.11

Lemma 3.1 and 3.2 in
Karp and Sipser [1981]
respectively establish the statements in
Lemma

D.11
(i) and (ii) for a general tree rooted at vertex
v
. As
\bar{G}
under consideration is also rooted at vertex
v
, these results immediately extend.

Proof of
Lemma

D.13

By
Lemma

D.11
(i), we have

\mathbb{P}\left[v\text{ is a target}|v\text{ is a flexible node in }S_{l}\right]=\mathbb{P}\left[v\in H|v\text{ is a flexible node in }S_{l}\right]=y_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b}).

To find the probability for a flexible node
v
in
S_{l}
to be a loser, we need to sum the probability that
v
is in
L
and that
v
has exactly
1
child which is not in
H
. The former is simply given by
y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})
, while the latter can be computed as

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[v\text{ has exactly 1 child that is not in }H|v\text{ is a flexible node in }S_{l}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\sum_{k=0}^{n}z^{f}_{k}(\mathbf{b})\cdot k\cdot\left(\frac{2b_{r}p^{f}_{n}}{(1+b_{r})p^{f}_{n}+(1-b_{r})p_{n}}\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+\frac{(1-b_{r})\cdot(p^{f}_{n}+p_{n})}{(1+b_{r})p^{f}_{n}+(1-b_{r})p_{n}}\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)^{k-1}

\displaystyle\cdot\left(1-\frac{2b_{r}p^{f}_{n}}{(1+b_{r})p^{f}_{n}+(1-b_{r})p_{n}}\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})-\frac{(1-b_{r})\cdot(p^{f}_{n}+p_{n})}{(1+b_{r})p^{f}_{n}+(1-b_{r})p_{n}}\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}\left((1+b_{r})p^{f}_{n}+(1-b_{r})p_{n}\right)^{k}\left(1-(1+b_{r})p^{f}_{n}-(1-b_{r})p_{n}\right)^{n-k}

\displaystyle\cdot k\cdot\left(\frac{2b_{r}p^{f}_{n}}{(1+b_{r})p^{f}_{n}+(1-b_{r})p_{n}}\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+\frac{(1-b_{r})\cdot(p^{f}_{n}+p_{n})}{(1+b_{r})p^{f}_{n}+(1-b_{r})p_{n}}\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)^{k-1}

\displaystyle\cdot\left(1-\frac{2b_{r}p^{f}_{n}}{(1+b_{r})p^{f}_{n}+(1-b_{r})p_{n}}\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})-\frac{(1-b_{r})\cdot(p^{f}_{n}+p_{n})}{(1+b_{r})p^{f}_{n}+(1-b_{r})p_{n}}\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\sum_{k=1}^{n}n\binom{n-1}{k-1}\left((1+b_{r})p^{f}_{n}+(1-b_{r})p_{n}\right)^{k-1}\left(1-(1+b_{r})p^{f}_{n}-(1-b_{r})p_{n}\right)^{n-k}

\displaystyle\cdot\left(\frac{2b_{r}p^{f}_{n}}{(1+b_{r})p^{f}_{n}+(1-b_{r})p_{n}}\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+\frac{(1-b_{r})\cdot(p^{f}_{n}+p_{n})}{(1+b_{r})p^{f}_{n}+(1-b_{r})p_{n}}\cdot\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)^{k-1}

\displaystyle\cdot\left((1+b_{r})p^{f}_{n}+(1-b_{r})p_{n}-2b_{r}p^{f}_{n}\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})-(1-b_{r})\cdot(p^{f}_{n}+p_{n})\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n-1}{k}\left((1+b_{r})p^{f}_{n}+(1-b_{r})p_{n}\right)^{k}\left(1-(1+b_{r})p^{f}_{n}-(1-b_{r})p_{n}\right)^{n-1-k}

\displaystyle\cdot\left(\frac{2b_{r}p^{f}_{n}}{(1+b_{r})p^{f}_{n}+(1-b_{r})p_{n}}\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+\frac{(1-b_{r})\cdot(p^{f}_{n}+p_{n})}{(1+b_{r})p^{f}_{n}+(1-b_{r})p_{n}}\cdot\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)^{k}\vskip 72.26999pt

\displaystyle\cdot n\cdot\left((1+b_{r})p^{f}_{n}+(1-b_{r})p_{n}-2b_{r}p^{f}_{n}\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})-(1-b_{r})\cdot(p^{f}_{n}+p_{n})\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n-1}{k}\left(1-(1+b_{r})p^{f}_{n}-(1-b_{r})p_{n}\right)^{n-1-k}\cdot\left(2b_{r}p^{f}_{n}\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+(1-b_{r})\cdot(p^{f}_{n}+p_{n})\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)^{k}

\displaystyle\cdot\left((1+b_{r})\alpha^{f}+(1-b_{r})\alpha-2b_{r}\alpha^{f}\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})-(1-b_{r})\cdot(\alpha^{f}+\alpha)\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\left(1-(1+b_{r})p^{f}_{n}-(1-b_{r})p_{n}+2b_{r}p^{f}_{n}\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+(1-b_{r})\cdot(p^{f}_{n}+p_{n})\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)^{n-1}

\displaystyle\cdot\left((1+b_{r})\alpha^{f}+(1-b_{r})\alpha-2b_{r}\alpha^{f}\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})-(1-b_{r})\cdot(\alpha^{f}+\alpha)\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\left[1-\frac{2b_{r}\alpha^{f}\left(1-\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\right)+(1-b_{r})\cdot(\alpha^{f}+\alpha)\left(1-\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)}{n}\right]^{n-1}

\displaystyle\cdot\left((1+b_{r})\alpha^{f}+(1-b_{r})\alpha-2b_{r}\alpha^{f}\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})-(1-b_{r})\cdot(\alpha^{f}+\alpha)\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;e^{-2b_{r}\alpha^{f}\left(1-\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\right)-(1-b_{r})\cdot(\alpha^{f}+\alpha)\left(1-\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)}

\displaystyle\cdot\left((1+b_{r})\alpha^{f}+(1-b_{r})\alpha-2b_{r}\alpha^{f}\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})-(1-b_{r})\cdot(\alpha^{f}+\alpha)\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)\text{ as }n\rightarrow\infty

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\left((1+b_{r})\alpha^{f}+(1-b_{r})\alpha-b_{r}2\alpha^{f}\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})-\left(1-b_{r}\right)\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)\text{ as }n\rightarrow\infty.

The third equality above follows from
k\binom{n}{k}=n\binom{n-1}{k-1}
, the fourth equality substitutes
k
with
k-1
everywhere and starts summation from
k=0
, and the sixth equality is an application of the Binomial Theorem.

Thus,
\mathbb{P}\left[v\text{ is a loser}|v\text{ is a flexible node in }S_{l}\right]
is given by

\displaystyle y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\left((1+b_{r})\alpha^{f}+(1-b_{r})\alpha-b_{r}2\alpha^{f}\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})-\left(1-b_{r}\right)\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right).

The probabilities conditional on
v
being a regular node or being from
S_{r}
can be derived analogously.
\square

Proof of
Proposition

D.14

Following closely the proof of Theorem
9
in
Karp and Sipser [1981]
, we start by showing that a random tree is a good approximation to the structure obtained by conducting a breadth-first search from
v.
Denote the subgraph of
G
induced by vertices at most distance
d
from
v
as the
d
-neighborhood of
v
. A vertex
v
is referred to as a
d
-target if there exists a derivation proving
v
to be a target within the
d
-neighborhood of
v
. Note that if
d-
neighborhood proves that
v
is a target then
v
is a target in any other graph that yields the same
d-
neighborhood.
Let

\displaystyle Y_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=

\displaystyle\;\mathbb{P}\left[v\text{ is a target in }G_{n}\mid v\text{ is a flexible node in }V_{l}\right],

\displaystyle Y^{d}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=

\displaystyle\;\mathbb{P}\left[v\text{ is a $d-$target in }G_{n}\mid v\text{ is a flexible node in }V_{l}\right],

\displaystyle Y^{d}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=

\displaystyle\;\mathbb{P}\left[v\text{ is a $d-$target root in }\bar{G}\mid v\text{ is a flexible node in }S_{l}\right].

Claim

6
shows that, for large
n
, the probability that a
d-
neighborhood occurs in a random graph approaches the probability of that
d-
neighborhood occurring in a random tree.

Claim 6

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}Y_{n}=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\lim_{d\rightarrow\infty}Y^{d}_{n}=\lim_{d\rightarrow\infty}\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}Y^{d}_{n}=\lim_{d\rightarrow\infty}Y^{d}=y_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b}).

That is,
\mathbb{P}\left[v\text{ is a target}\mid v\text{ is a flexible node in }V_{l}\right]=y_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})
as
n\rightarrow\infty
. Similarly, the probabilities for
v
to be a target or a loser, when
v\in V_{l}
or
V_{r},
and when
v
is a flexible or regular node, follow those derived for random trees in
Lemma

D.13
.

Since all members of derivations are targets or losers or both, we next find the probability for a random edge
(v,u)
in
G
to satisfy both
v\bigotimes u
and
u\bigotimes v
i.e.,
v
is both a target and a loser. By Theorem 9 (3) in
Karp and Sipser [1981]
, in
\bar{G}
this occurs if and only if both
u
and
v
are in
L
. We compute this probability conditional on the types of root nodes
v
, and the extension from
\bar{G}
to
G
follows from
Claim

6
.

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[v\bigotimes u\text{ and }u\bigotimes v\mid v\text{ is a flexible node in }V_{l}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\mathbb{P}\left[v,u\text{ are both in }L\mid v\text{ is a flexible node in }V_{l}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\mathbb{P}\left[u\text{ is a flexible node}\mid(v,u)\in E\right]\hat{y}_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\mathbb{P}\left[u\text{ is a regular node}\mid(v,u)\in E\right]\hat{y}_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\frac{b_{r}2\alpha^{f}}{(1+b_{r})\alpha^{f}+(1-b_{r})\alpha}\hat{y}_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\frac{(1-b_{r})(\alpha^{f}+\alpha)}{(1+b_{r})\alpha^{f}+(1-b_{r})\alpha}\hat{y}_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b}).

Similarly, we find

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[v\bigotimes u\text{ and }u\bigotimes v\mid v\text{ is a regular node in }V_{l}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;y_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\frac{b_{r}(\alpha^{f}+\alpha)}{b_{r}\alpha^{f}+(2-b_{r})\alpha}\hat{y}_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+y_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\frac{(1-b_{r})2\alpha}{b_{r}\alpha^{f}+(2-b_{r})\alpha}\hat{y}_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b}),

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[v\bigotimes u\text{ and }u\bigotimes v\mid v\text{ is a flexible node in }V_{r}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\hat{y}_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\frac{b_{l}2\alpha^{f}}{(1+b_{l})\alpha^{f}+(1-b_{l})\alpha}y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+\hat{y}_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\frac{(1-b_{l})(\alpha^{f}+\alpha)}{(1+b_{l})\alpha^{f}+(1-b_{l})\alpha}y_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b}),

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[v\bigotimes u\text{ and }u\bigotimes v\mid v\text{ is a regular node in }V_{r}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\hat{y}_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\frac{b_{l}(\alpha^{f}+\alpha)}{b_{l}\alpha^{f}+(2-b_{l})\alpha}y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+\hat{y}_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\frac{(1-b_{l})2\alpha}{b_{l}\alpha^{f}+(2-b_{l})\alpha}y_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b}).

Thus,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[v\text{ in a derivation}\mid v\text{ is a flexible node in }V_{l}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[v\text{ is a target}\mid v\text{ is a flexible node in }V_{l}\right]+\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[v\text{ is a loser}\mid v\text{ is a flexible node in }V_{l}\right]

\displaystyle-\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[v\bigotimes u\text{ and }u\bigotimes v\mid v\text{ is a flexible node in }V_{l}\right]\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\left|u\text{ s.t. }(v,u)\in E\right|\biggr\lvert v\text{ is a flexible node in }V_{l}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;y_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\left((1+b_{r})\alpha^{f}+(1-b_{r})\alpha-b_{r}2\alpha^{f}\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})-\left(1-b_{r}\right)\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)

\displaystyle-\left[y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\frac{b_{r}2\alpha^{f}}{(1+b_{r})\alpha^{f}+(1-b_{r})\alpha}\hat{y}_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\frac{(1-b_{r})(\alpha^{f}+\alpha)}{(1+b_{r})\alpha^{f}+(1-b_{r})\alpha}\hat{y}_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right]\cdot\left((1+b_{r})\alpha^{f}+(1-b_{r})\alpha\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle y_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+y_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\left[(1+b_{r})\alpha^{f}+(1-b_{r})\alpha\right]\cdot

\displaystyle\left[1-\frac{b_{r}2\alpha^{f}}{(1+b_{r})\alpha^{f}+(1-b_{r})\alpha}\left(\hat{y}_{L}^{f}(\mathbf{b})+\hat{y}_{H}^{f}(\mathbf{b})\right)-\frac{(1-b_{r})\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)}{(1+b_{r})\alpha^{f}+(1-b_{r})\alpha}\left(\hat{y}_{L}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})+\hat{y}_{H}^{nf}(\mathbf{b})\right)\right].

The probabilities conditional on other types of nodes are computed similarly.
\square

Proof D.21

Proof of
Claim

6

The first and last equalities in the claim arise directly from our definitions and
Lemma

D.13
. We now prove the third and the second equality. By the Poisson Limit Theorem, for any flexible node
v\in V_{l},
the distribution of its degrees follows
\text{Poisson}\left(b_{r}2\alpha^{f}+(1-b_{r})(\alpha^{f}+\alpha)\right)
as
n\to\infty.
Thus, for any
\epsilon>0
there is a constant
k
such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[\text{there is a node of degree $>k$ in the $d-$neighborhood of $v$}\right]<\epsilon.
The rest of the analyses exactly follow the proof of Theorem 9 (1) in
Karp and Sipser [1981]
, which shows
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}Y^{d}_{n}=Y^{d}
because (i) there are finitely many
d-
neighborhoods that lack a vertex of degree
>k
, and (ii) the probability of encountering each such
d-
neighborhood around a node
v
in
G_{n}
is close to that around a root
v
in
\bar{G}
as
n\to\infty
. This proves the third equality.

Finally, we justify the limit exchange in the second equality. We leverage the result from Theorem 9 (1) in
Karp and Sipser [1981]
that, for every positive
\epsilon
, there exists a
d
such that for all sufficiently large
n
,
\mathbb{P}\left[v\text{ is a target but not a $d$-target}\right]<\epsilon
.
14
14

14

This result relies on probabilistic bounds on the length of a shortest derivation that proves
v
to be a target.
This implies that for all sufficiently large
n,n^{\prime}d,d^{\prime},Y^{d}_{n}
is close to
Y^{d^{\prime}}_{n^{\prime}}
and thus we may exchange limit.

Proof of
Claim

2

First, we calculate the derivative of
f_{1}(x_{1})
:

\displaystyle f_{1}^{\prime}(x_{1})=

\displaystyle\;\frac{\partial e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)x_{1}+2\frac{\alpha}{\alpha^{f}+\alpha}\left(\log(x_{1})+\alpha^{f}x_{1}\right)}+2\frac{\log(x_{1})+\alpha^{f}x_{1}}{\alpha^{f}+\alpha}}{\partial x_{1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)x_{1}+2\frac{\alpha}{\alpha^{f}+\alpha}\left(\log(x_{1})+\alpha^{f}x_{1}\right)}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)+2\frac{\alpha}{\alpha^{f}+\alpha}\left(\alpha^{f}+1/x_{1}\right)\right)+\frac{2}{\alpha^{f}+\alpha}\left(\alpha^{f}+1/x_{1}\right).

We next employ a computer-aided proof to verify that
f_{1}^{\prime}(x_{1})>1
for any
x_{1}\in(0,1]
when
10^{-4}<\alpha<\alpha^{f}
and
\alpha^{f}+\alpha<e
. Fixing
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}>0
, we establish a lower bound on the value of
f_{1}^{\prime}(x_{1})
for any
\left(\bar{\alpha}^{f},\bar{\alpha},\bar{x}_{1}\right)
in the set
[\alpha^{f},\alpha^{f}+\delta_{1})\times[\alpha,\alpha+\delta_{1})\times[x_{1},x_{1}+\delta_{2})
, where
10^{-4}<\alpha<\alpha^{f}
and
\alpha^{f}+\alpha<e
. We find that:

\begin{split}f_{1}^{\prime}(x_{1})\geq&\ e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)x_{1}+\frac{2\left(\alpha^{f}+\delta_{1}\right)\left(\alpha+\delta_{1}\right)\left(x_{1}+\delta_{2}\right)}{\alpha^{f}+\alpha}+\frac{2\alpha\log(x_{1}+\delta_{2})}{\alpha^{f}+\alpha+2\delta_{1}}}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha+2\delta_{1}\right)\right)\\
&+e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha+2\delta_{1}\right)\left(x_{1}+\delta_{2}\right)+\frac{2\alpha^{f}\alpha x_{1}}{\alpha^{f}+\alpha+2\delta_{1}}+\frac{2\left(\alpha+\delta_{1}\right)\log(x_{1})}{\alpha^{f}+\alpha}}\left(\frac{2\alpha\left(\alpha^{f}+\frac{1}{x_{1}+\delta_{2}}\right)}{\alpha^{f}+\alpha+2\delta_{1}}\right)\\
&+\frac{2\left(\alpha^{f}+\frac{1}{x_{1}+\delta_{2}}\right)}{\alpha^{f}+\alpha+2\delta_{1}}\text{ when }x_{1}>0,\\
f_{1}^{\prime}(x_{1})\geq&\ e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha\right)x_{1}+\frac{2\left(\alpha^{f}+\delta_{1}\right)\left(\alpha+\delta_{1}\right)\left(x_{1}+\delta_{2}\right)}{\alpha^{f}+\alpha}+\frac{2\alpha\log(x_{1}+\delta_{2})}{\alpha^{f}+\alpha+2\delta_{1}}}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\left(\alpha^{f}+\alpha+2\delta_{1}\right)\right)\\
&+\frac{2\left(\alpha^{f}+\frac{1}{x_{1}+\delta_{2}}\right)}{\alpha^{f}+\alpha+2\delta_{1}}\text{ when }x_{1}=0.\end{split}

(45)

For given
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}>0
, if the lower bound in (
45
) is greater than
1
, then
f_{1}^{\prime}(x_{1})>1
for any
(\bar{\alpha}^{f},\bar{\alpha},\bar{x}_{1})
in the corresponding set
[\alpha^{f},\alpha^{f}+\delta_{1})\times[\alpha,\alpha+\delta_{1})\times[x_{1},x_{1}+\delta_{2})
. In the computational notebook titled
Claim3.ipynb
,
15
15

15

The computer-aided proof can be found at
https://bit.ly/3VhfumL
.
we compute the value of (
45
) for
x_{1}=0,\delta_{2},2\delta_{2},\ldots,1
and
\alpha^{f},\alpha=10^{-4},\delta_{1},2\delta_{1},\ldots,e
, under the constraint
\alpha^{f}+\alpha<e
. Through this computation, we find that taking
\delta_{1}=\delta_{2}=0.01
is sufficient for verifying (
45
)
>1
for all
x_{1}\in(0,1],10^{-4}<\alpha<\alpha^{f}
and
\alpha^{f}+\alpha<e
.
\square

D.5.6
Computational results based on
\mu^{\texttt{KS}}(b_{l},b_{r})
.

To compute
\mu^{\texttt{KS}}(b_{l},b_{r})
, we resort to the
NLsolve
package available in the Julia Programming Language to solve (
14
) at a tolerance level
ftol=10^{-8}
. Specifically,
NLsolve
iteratively refines candidate solutions using the Trust Region Method until the infinite norm of the residuals of the current solution falls below the threshold
ftol=10^{-8}

[
NLsolve, 2017
]
. When
10^{-4}<\alpha<\alpha^{f}
and
\alpha^{f}+\alpha<e
, at
\mathbf{b}=(1/2,1/2),(1,0)
and
(0,1)
we find that (
14
) reduces to a single non-linear equation that exhibits strict monotonicity on both sides and can be solved to provable precision using this method (see the proof of
Theorem

D.6
for details). In other cases where there are no theoretical guarantees for the closeness of
\mu^{\texttt{KS}}(b_{l},b_{r})
to
\mu(b_{l},b_{r})
, numerical studies indicate that
\mu^{\texttt{EMP}}_{n}(b_{l},b_{r})
still tends to converge to
\mu^{\texttt{KS}}(b_{l},b_{r})
as
n
scales large. We thus employ
\mu^{\texttt{KS}}(b_{l},b_{r})
to compare the one-sided and the balanced allocations for a wide range of parameters. Specifically, the findings presented in
Section

5
are based on values of
\left(B,b_{l},\alpha^{f},\alpha\right)
in a set
S
that contains all
B\in\left\{0.1,0.2,\cdots,1\right\},b_{l}\in\left\{0,0.01,\cdots,1\right\},\alpha^{f}\in\left\{0.05,0.10,\cdots,7.45,7.50\right\}
and
\alpha\in\left\{0,0.05,0.10,\cdots,2.95,3.00\right\}
such that
B\geq b_{l}
and
\alpha^{f}>\alpha
.

Appendix E
Degree-0 Metric (Proof of
Theorem

2.2
)

We first derive the closed-form expression for
\phi(b_{l},b_{r})
, then prove that it always takes a maximum at either
(B,0)
or
\left(B/2,B/2\right)
, and finally compare the two solutions.

Step 1: Deriving
\phi(b_{l},b_{r})
.

For a node
v^{l}_{i}\in V_{l}
, the probability of being isolated is the probability that no edge realizes to any of the
n
right nodes. Conditioning on its flexibility type, this equals

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\deg(v_{i}^{l})=0\right]

\displaystyle=\;\underbrace{b_{l}}_{\text{$v^{l}_{i}$ is flexible}}\Big(\underbrace{b_{r}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}v^{r}_{j}\text{ is flexible}\end{subarray}}\Bigl(1-\tfrac{2\alpha^{f}}{n}\Bigr)+\underbrace{(1-b_{r})}_{\begin{subarray}{c}v^{r}_{j}\text{ is regular}\end{subarray}}\Bigl(1-\tfrac{\alpha^{f}+\alpha}{n}\Bigr)\Big)^{n}

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{(1-b_{l})}_{\text{$v^{l}_{i}$ is regular}}\Big(\underbrace{b_{r}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}v^{r}_{j}\text{ is flexible}\end{subarray}}\Bigl(1-\tfrac{\alpha^{f}+\alpha}{n}\Bigr)+\underbrace{(1-b_{r})}_{\begin{subarray}{c}v^{r}_{j}\text{ is regular}\end{subarray}}\Bigl(1-\tfrac{2\alpha}{n}\Bigr)\Big)^{n}

\displaystyle=\;b_{l}\Bigl(1-\tfrac{2\alpha^{f}b_{r}+(\alpha^{f}+\alpha)(1-b_{r})}{n}\Bigr)^{n}+(1-b_{l})\Bigl(1-\tfrac{(\alpha^{f}+\alpha)b_{r}+2\alpha(1-b_{r})}{n}\Bigr)^{n}.

By symmetry, the expected isolated fraction on the right is obtained by swapping
b_{l}
and
b_{r}
.

Taking
n\to\infty
and using
(1-x/n)^{n}\to e^{-x}
gives

\displaystyle\phi_{1}(b_{l},b_{r})

\displaystyle=b_{l}\,e^{-\bigl(2\alpha^{f}b_{r}+(\alpha+\alpha^{f})(1-b_{r})\bigr)}+(1-b_{l})\,e^{-\bigl((\alpha+\alpha^{f})b_{r}+2\alpha(1-b_{r})\bigr)},

\displaystyle\phi_{2}(b_{l},b_{r})

\displaystyle=b_{r}\,e^{-\bigl(2\alpha^{f}b_{l}+(\alpha+\alpha^{f})(1-b_{l})\bigr)}+(1-b_{r})\,e^{-\bigl((\alpha+\alpha^{f})b_{l}+2\alpha(1-b_{l})\bigr)}.

Thus, the limiting maximum fraction of isolated nodes is
\max\{\phi_{1}(b_{l},b_{r}),\phi_{2}(b_{l},b_{r})\}
, and therefore

\phi(b_{l},b_{r})=1-\max\{\phi_{1}(b_{l},b_{r}),\ \phi_{2}(b_{l},b_{r})\}.

Step 2: Optimality occurs at either the balanced or the one-sided allocation.
Let
d\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\alpha^{f}-\alpha
and
x\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=e^{-d}>0
. If
d=0,
we trivially have
\phi(b_{l},B-b_{l})=\phi(b_{l}^{\prime},B-b_{l}^{\prime})\;\forall b_{l},b_{l}^{\prime}\in[0,B],
so both the balanced and the one-sided allocations are optimal. Thus, we focus on the case where
d>0
, i.e.,
\alpha^{f}>\alpha.

We start by showing that
\phi_{1}(b_{l},B-b_{l})
is monotonically increasing in
b_{l}\in[0,b_{l}^{\star}]
for some
b_{l}^{\star}\geq B/2
and monotonically decreasing thereafter. This property will later allow us to prove the optimality of the two special allocations. First, a direct algebraic rearrangement yields that
the two exponentials appearing in
\phi_{1}(b_{l},B-b_{l})
have a constant ratio (independent of
b_{l}
):

\frac{e^{-\,[2\alpha^{f}(B-b_{l})+(\alpha+\alpha^{f})(1-B+b_{l})]}}{e^{-\,[\,(\alpha+\alpha^{f})(B-b_{l})+2\alpha(1-B+b_{l})\,]}}=e^{-(\alpha^{f}-\alpha)}\;=\;x.

Hence we can factor one exponential and write

\phi_{1}(b_{l},B-b_{l})=C_{B}\,e^{db_{l}}\,\Big(1-(1-x)\,b_{l}\Big),\qquad\text{where}\quad C_{B}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\exp\!\big(-[(\alpha+\alpha^{f})B+2\alpha(1-B)]\big)>0.

(46)

Differentiating (
46
) with respect to
b_{l}
gives

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\phi_{1}(b_{l},B-b_{l})}{\partial b_{l}}

\displaystyle=C_{B}\,e^{db_{l}}\Big(d\big(1-(1-x)b_{l}\big)-(1-x)\Big).

(47)

Since
C_{B}\,e^{db_{l}}>0
, the sign of
\frac{\partial\phi_{1}(b_{l},B-b_{l})}{\partial b_{l}}
is determined by the
affine

function

\;d\big(1-(1-x)b_{l}\big)-(1-x)\;=\;-d(1-x)\,b_{l}\;+\;\big(d-(1-x)\big)

As the second summand is independent of
b_{l}
, this function is linear in
b_{l}
, and its first-order condition yields:

b_{l}^{\star}\;=\;\min\left\{\frac{d-(1-x)}{d(1-x)},B\right\},\qquad\text{whenever }d\neq 0.

(48)

This proves that
\phi_{1}(b_{l},B-b_{l})
is monotone increasing in
b_{l}\in[0,b_{l}^{\star}]
and monotone decreasing thereafter.

We next show that
\frac{d-(1-x)}{d(1-x)}\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\geq\tfrac{B}{2}
. Since
B\geq B/2,
this would immediately imply that
b_{l}^{\star}\geq\tfrac{B}{2}
. Using
x=e^{-d}
and simple algebra,

\frac{d-(1-x)}{d(1-x)}\;\geq\;\tfrac{1}{2}\iff 2\big(d-(1-x)\big)\;\geq\;d(1-x)\iff 2d-(2+d)(1-x)\;\geq\;0.

Since
x=e^{-d}\geq\frac{2-d}{2+d}
for any
d\geq 0
, we have

(2+d)(1-x)\;\leq\;(2+d)\Big(1-\frac{2-d}{2+d}\Big)\;=\;2d,

which proves
2d-(2+d)(1-x)\geq 0
and thus
b_{l}^{\star}\geq\tfrac{B}{2}
.

We next leverage these properties of
\phi_{1}(b_{l},B-b_{l})
to prove that optimality of
\phi(b_{l},B-b_{l})
occurs at either the balanced or the one-sided allocation. As
b_{l}^{\star}\leq B,
from
\phi_{2}(b_{l},B-b_{l})=\phi_{1}(B-b_{l},b_{l})
we obtain

\max\left\{\phi_{1}(b_{l},B-b_{l}),\phi_{2}(b_{l},B-b_{l})\right\}=\phi_{1}(b_{l},B-b_{l})\;\forall b_{l}\in[B/2,b_{l}^{\star}].

By the monotonicity of
\phi_{1}(b_{l},B-b_{l})
in this same region, we conclude that

\max\left\{\phi_{1}(B/2,B/2),\phi_{2}(B/2,B/2)\right\}\leq\max\left\{\phi_{1}(b_{l},B-b_{l}),\phi_{2}(b_{l},B-b_{l})\right\}\;\forall b_{l}\in[B/2,b_{l}^{\star}]

and thus
\phi(B/2,B/2)\geq\phi(b_{l},B-b_{l})\;\forall b_{l}\in[B/2,b_{l}^{\star}]
. By symmetry of the function
\phi(b_{l},B-b_{l})
around
(B/2,B/2)
, we obtain:

\displaystyle\phi(B/2,B/2)\geq\phi(b_{l},B-b_{l})\;\forall b_{l}\in[B-b_{l}^{\star},b_{l}^{\star}].

(49)

Also, since both
\phi_{1}(b_{l},B-b_{l})
and
\phi_{2}(b_{l},B-b_{l})
are monotonically decreasing for
b_{l}\in[b_{l}^{\star},B]
, we have

\max\left\{\phi_{1}(0,B),\phi_{2}(0,B)\right\}\leq\max\left\{\phi_{1}(b_{l},B-b_{l}),\phi_{2}(b_{l},B-b_{l})\right\}\;\forall b_{l}\in[b_{l}^{\star},B].

Combined with the symmetry of function
\phi(b_{l},B-b_{l})
, we obtain

\displaystyle\phi(B,0)=\phi(0,B)\geq\phi(b_{l},B-b_{l})\;\forall b_{l}\in[0,B-b_{l}^{\star}]\cup[b_{l}^{\star},B].

(50)

Combining (
49
) and (
50
), we find that one of
(B/2,B/2)
or
(B,0)
and
(0,B)
optimizes
\phi(b_{l},B-~b_{l})
.

Step 3: Comparing one-sided and balanced allocations.
It suffices to then compare the one-sided and the balanced allocations. By the definition of
\phi
,

\phi(B,0)\ \geq\ \phi\!\left(\tfrac{B}{2},\tfrac{B}{2}\right)\iff\max\big\{\phi_{1}(B,0),\ \phi_{2}(B,0)\big\}\ \leq\ \phi_{1}\!\left(\tfrac{B}{2},\tfrac{B}{2}\right).

(51)

A direct substitution gives

\displaystyle\phi_{1}(B,0)

\displaystyle=B\,e^{-(\alpha+\alpha^{f})}+(1-B)\,e^{-2\alpha},

\displaystyle\phi_{2}(B,0)

\displaystyle=e^{-\left(2\alpha+B(\alpha^{f}-\alpha)\right)}=e^{-2\alpha}x^{B},

\displaystyle\phi_{1}\!\Big(\tfrac{B}{2},\tfrac{B}{2}\Big)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{B}{2}\,e^{-\left(\alpha^{f}B+(\alpha+\alpha^{f})(1-\tfrac{B}{2})\right)}+\Big(1-\tfrac{B}{2}\Big)\,e^{-\left((\alpha+\alpha^{f})\tfrac{B}{2}+2\alpha(1-\tfrac{B}{2})\right)}=e^{-2\alpha}x^{B/2}\left[\left(1-B/2\right)+B/2\cdot x\right].

Thus, the inequality

\phi_{2}(B,0)\leq\phi_{1}(\tfrac{B}{2},\tfrac{B}{2})

is equivalent to

x^{B/2}\ \leq\ \Big(1-\tfrac{B}{2}\Big)+\tfrac{B}{2}\,x,

which holds for any
\alpha^{f}\geq\alpha
and
B
. Hence from (
51
) we obtain the simplification

\phi(B,0)\ \geq\ \phi\!\left(\tfrac{B}{2},\tfrac{B}{2}\right)\iff\phi_{1}(B,0)\ \leq\ \phi_{1}\!\left(\tfrac{B}{2},\tfrac{B}{2}\right).

(52)

With
x=e^{-(\alpha^{f}-\alpha)}
,

\phi_{1}(B,0)=e^{-2\alpha}\big[(1-B)+Bx\big],\qquad\phi_{1}\!\Big(\tfrac{B}{2},\tfrac{B}{2}\Big)=e^{-2\alpha}\,x^{B/2}\!\Big[\Big(1-\tfrac{B}{2}\Big)+\tfrac{B}{2}x\Big].

As
e^{-2\alpha}>0
, (
52
) is equivalent to

(1-B)+Bx\ \leq\ x^{B/2}\Big(1-\tfrac{B}{2}+\tfrac{B}{2}x\Big),
so for
B\in(0,1]
and any
(\alpha,\alpha^{f})
,

\phi(B,0)\ \geq\ \phi\!\left(\tfrac{B}{2},\tfrac{B}{2}\right)\iff(1-B)+B\,e^{-(\alpha^{f}-\alpha)}\ \leq\ e^{-\frac{B}{2}(\alpha^{f}-\alpha)}\!\left(1-\tfrac{B}{2}+\tfrac{B}{2}\,e^{-(\alpha^{f}-\alpha)}\right)\iff\phi^{\star}\left(B,\alpha^{f},\alpha\right)\geq 0.

where
\phi^{\star}\left(B,\alpha^{f},\alpha\right)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=e^{-\frac{B}{2}(\alpha^{f}-\alpha)}\!\left(1-\tfrac{B}{2}+\tfrac{B}{2}\,e^{-(\alpha^{f}-\alpha)}\right)-(1-B)-B\,e^{-(\alpha^{f}-\alpha)}.

Step 4: The balanced allocation always dominates when
B\leq 2/3
.
Consider the auxiliary univariate function on
(0,1]
,

h_{B}(u)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=u^{B/2}\Bigl(1-\tfrac{B}{2}+\tfrac{B}{2}\,u\Bigr)-(1-B)-B\,u,\qquad u\in(0,1],

\text{so that}\qquad\phi^{\star}\left(B,\alpha^{f},\alpha\right)=h_{B}\!\left(e^{-(\alpha^{f}-\alpha)}\right).

Note that
h_{B}(1)=0
and
\lim_{u\downarrow 0}h_{B}(u)=B-1<0
for all
B\in(0,1]
.

Taking the first- and second-order derivatives of
h_{B}(u)
yields:

h_{B}^{\prime}(u)=\tfrac{B}{2}u^{B/2-1}\Bigl(1-\tfrac{B}{2}+\tfrac{B}{2}u\Bigr)+u^{B/2}\cdot\tfrac{B}{2}-B,\text{ and}

\displaystyle h_{B}^{\prime\prime}(u)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{B}{2}\!\left(\tfrac{B}{2}-1\right)u^{B/2-2}\Bigl(1-\tfrac{B}{2}+\tfrac{B}{2}u\Bigr)+\tfrac{B}{2}u^{B/2-1}\cdot\tfrac{B}{2}+\tfrac{B}{2}\!\left(\tfrac{B}{2}\right)u^{B/2-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{B}{8}\,u^{B/2-2}\,\Bigl((B^{2}+2B)\,u+(-B^{2}+4B-4)\Bigr).

For each fixed
B>0
, the bracket is affine and increasing in
u
; thus for
u\in(0,1]
it is upper bounded by its value at
u=1
:

(B^{2}+2B)\,u+(-B^{2}+4B-4)\ \leq\ 6B-4.

\text{Hence,
}\qquad B\leq\tfrac{2}{3}\ \Longrightarrow\ h_{B}^{\prime\prime}(u)\leq 0\quad\text{for all }u\in(0,1],

i.e.,
h_{B}
is concave on
(0,1]
whenever
B\leq\tfrac{2}{3}
. Because
h_{B}
is concave and continuous on
[0,1]
with
h_{B}(1)=0
and
h_{B}(0^{+})=B-1<0
, its maximum over
[0,1]
equals
0
and is attained at
u=1
. Therefore,

B\leq\tfrac{2}{3}\ \Longrightarrow\ h_{B}(u)\leq 0\quad\text{for all }u\in(0,1].

Evaluating at
u=e^{-(\alpha^{f}-\alpha)}\in(0,1]
yields

B\leq\tfrac{2}{3}\ \Longrightarrow\ \phi^{\star}\left(B,\alpha^{f},\alpha\right)\leq 0\ \Longrightarrow\ \phi(B,0)\ \leq\ \phi\!\left(\tfrac{B}{2},\tfrac{B}{2}\right)\quad\text{for all }(\alpha^{f},\alpha).

In contrast, when
B>\tfrac{2}{3}
, we have:

h_{B}(1)=0,\qquad h_{B}^{\prime}(1)=0,\qquad h_{B}^{\prime\prime}(1)=\frac{B(6B-4)}{8}>0,

so
u=1
is a strict local
minimum
of
h_{B}
. Therefore there exists
u\in(0,1)
sufficiently close to
1
with
h_{B}(u)>0
. Since every such
u
can be written as
u=e^{-(\alpha^{f}-\alpha)}
for some
(\alpha^{f},\alpha)
, we obtain

\phi^{\star}\left(B,\alpha^{f},\alpha\right)=h_{B}\!\left(e^{-(\alpha^{f}-\alpha)}\right)>0

for a suitable choice of
(\alpha^{f},\alpha)
. This shows that
\phi(B,0)\ \leq\ \phi\!\left(\tfrac{B}{2},\tfrac{B}{2}\right)
for all
(\alpha^{f},\alpha)
if and only if
B\leq 2/3.

Step 5: The one-sided allocation always dominates when
B=1
.

At
B=1
, the optimality condition for the one-sided allocation reduces to

e^{-\left(\alpha^{f}-\alpha\right)}\leq\frac{1}{2}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(\alpha^{f}-\alpha\right)}\left(1+e^{-\left(\alpha^{f}-\alpha\right)}\right).

We divide both sides by
e^{-\left(\alpha^{f}-\alpha\right)/2}>0
to obtain

e^{-\left(\alpha^{f}-\alpha\right)/2}\ \leq\ \tfrac{1}{2}\Big(1+e^{-\left(\alpha^{f}-\alpha\right)}\Big).

Defining the auxiliary variable
y\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=e^{-\left(\alpha^{f}-\alpha\right)/2}\in(0,1]
, this is equivalent to
y\ \leq\ \tfrac{1}{2}\big(1+y^{2}\big)
,
which always holds because
\tfrac{1}{2}\big(y^{2}-2y+1\big)\;=\;\tfrac{1}{2}\,(y-1)^{2}\geq 0.
Thus, at
B=1
we have

\phi(B,0)=\max_{b_{l}\in[0,B]}\phi(b_{l},B-b_{l}),\forall\alpha^{f}\geq\alpha,B\in[0,1].\qquad\square

Appendix F
Proofs of the Local Model

In this section, we prove the results for the local model. We derive a closed-form expression for
\mu(b_{l},b_{r})
as a rational function, in which both the denominator and numerator are eighth-order polynomials in terms of
b_{l}
,
b_{r}
,
p^{f}
, and
p
. This expression facilitates the comparison between one-sided and balanced allocation, as well as the analysis of the convexity and concavity properties of
\mu(b_{l},b_{r})
along specific diagonals where analyses of directional second-order derivatives are tractable.

F.1
Proof of
Theorem

7.1

We begin by characterizing the asymptotic fraction of nodes matched in the local model. Let
E
denote the set of all edges. We propose
Algorithm

3
to construct a matching
M
, and argue that it is at most
1
below the size of a maximum matching (thus, the matching probability under
Algorithm

3
is the same, asymptotically, as that under a maximum matching).

Algorithm 3

Maximum Matching Construction in Local Model

1:

Initialize the matching set
M\leftarrow\emptyset

2:

if

\left(v^{l}_{1},v^{r}_{1}\right)\in E

then

3:

Add
\left(v^{l}_{1},v^{r}_{1}\right)
to
M

4:

for
each subsequent node
v^{r}_{i}
with
i>1

do

5:

if

\left(v^{l}_{i-1},v^{r}_{i}\right)\in E
and
v^{l}_{i-1}
is not already matched in
M

then

6:

Add
\left(v^{l}_{i-1},v^{r}_{i}\right)
to
M

7:

else

if

\left(v^{l}_{i},v^{r}_{i}\right)\in E

then

8:

Add
\left(v^{l}_{i},v^{r}_{i}\right)
to
M

9:

return

M

Our analysis focuses on the nodes in
V_{r}
that are being matched through
Algorithm

3
, as opposed to the ones that could be matched in a maximum matching. Recall that each node
v^{l}_{i}
in
V_{l}
can only connect to its two neighbors in
V_{r}
. If
(v^{l}_{i},v^{r}_{i})\notin E
and
(v^{l}_{i},v^{r}_{i+1})\in E
, then there exists a maximum matching that contains
(v^{l}_{i},v^{r}_{i+1})
. This is because node
v^{l}_{i}
cannot be matched otherwise, and not using
(v^{l}_{i},v^{r}_{i+1})
in the matching would at most save
v^{r}_{i+1}
for one additional match. Hence, this algorithm is provably optimal for all nodes in
V_{r}
except
v^{r}_{1}
, which is myopically matched to
v^{l}_{1}
if an edge exists. As we are interested in computing
\mu(b_{l},b_{r})=\lim_{{n\to\infty}}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\mathcal{M}_{n}(b_{l},b_{r})}{n}\right]
, the resulting asymptotic error in
\mu(b_{l},b_{r})
approaches
0
.

Therefore, to compute
\mu(b_{l},b_{r})
it is sufficient to calculate the asymptotic fraction of nodes matched through this algorithm. Observe that whether a node
v^{r}_{i}\in V_{r}
is matched to
v^{l}_{i-1}\in V_{l}
depends only on edges incident to
v^{l}_{i-1}
and is independent of
v^{l}_{i}
. For each
v^{r}_{i}
with
i\in\{2,...,n\},
we define

\begin{split}x^{f}_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\;\mathbb{P}\left[\left(v^{l}_{i-1},v^{r}_{i}\right)\in M\mid v^{r}_{i}\text{ is flexible}\right]\text{ and }x^{n}_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\;\mathbb{P}\left[\left(v^{l}_{i-1},v^{r}_{i}\right)\in M\mid v^{r}_{i}\text{ is regular}\right].\end{split}

We now establish a system of equations to compute
(x^{f}_{i+1},x^{n}_{i+1})
based on
(x^{f}_{i},x^{n}_{i})
, which hold for any
i\in\{2,...,n\}.

To compute
x^{f}_{i+1}
, we consider all possible scenarios in which
v^{r}_{i+1}
gets matched to
v^{l}_{i}
: (1)
\left(v^{l}_{i-1},v^{r}_{i}\right)\in M
and
\left(v^{l}_{i},v^{r}_{i+1}\right)\in E
, or (2)
\left(v^{l}_{i-1},v^{r}_{i}\right)\notin M
,
\left(v^{l}_{i},v^{r}_{i}\right)\notin E
and
\left(v^{l}_{i},v^{r}_{i+1}\right)\in E
. Suppose
v^{l}_{i}
and
v^{r}_{i}
are both flexible nodes; if
v^{r}_{i+1}
is also flexible then (1) occurs
w.p.

x^{f}_{i}2p^{f}
and (2) occurs
w.p.

(1-x^{f}_{i})(1-2p^{f})2p^{f}
.
Thus, conditioned on
v^{l}_{i}
,
v^{r}_{i}
, and
v^{r}_{i+1}
all being flexible nodes,
\left(v^{l}_{i},v^{r}_{i+1}\right)\in M
with probability
x^{f}_{i}2p^{f}+(1-x^{f}_{i})(1-2p^{f})2p^{f}
.

For the cases where
v^{l}_{i}
or
v^{r}_{i}
are of other node types, we compute (conditioned on node types) the respective probabilities of the events

\left\{\left\{\left(v^{l}_{i-1},v^{r}_{i}\right)\in M\right\}\cap\left\{\left(v^{l}_{i},v^{r}_{i+1}\right)\in E\right\}\right\}\text{ and }\left\{\left\{\left(v^{l}_{i-1},v^{r}_{i}\right)\notin M\right\}\cap\left\{\left(v^{l}_{i},v^{r}_{i}\right)\notin E\right\}\cap\left\{\left(v^{l}_{i},v^{r}_{i+1}\right)\in E\right\}\right\}

accordingly and find that:

\begin{split}x^{f}_{i+1}=&\;f_{1}(x^{f}_{i},x^{n}_{i})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=b_{l}b_{r}\left[x^{f}_{i}2p^{f}+(1-x^{f}_{i})(1-2p^{f})2p^{f}\right]+(1-b_{l})b_{r}\left[x^{f}_{i}(p^{f}+p)+(1-x^{f}_{i})(1-p^{f}-p)(p^{f}+p)\right]\\
&+b_{l}(1-b_{r})\left[x^{n}_{i}2p^{f}+(1-x^{n}_{i})(1-p^{f}-p)2p^{f}\right]+(1-b_{l})(1-b_{r})\left[x^{n}_{i}(p^{f}+p)+(1-x^{n}_{i})(1-2p)(p^{f}+p)\right].\end{split}

(53)

Similarly, for
x^{n}_{i+1}
we find that:

\begin{split}x^{n}_{i+1}=&\;f_{2}(x^{f}_{i},x^{n}_{i})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=b_{l}b_{r}\left[x^{f}_{i}(p^{f}+p)+(1-x^{f}_{i})(1-2p^{f})(p^{f}+p)\right]+(1-b_{l})b_{r}\left[x^{f}_{i}2p+(1-x^{f}_{i})(1-p^{f}-p)2p\right]\\
&+b_{l}(1-b_{r})\left[x^{n}_{i}(p^{f}+p)+(1-x^{n}_{i})(1-p^{f}-p)(p^{f}+p)\right]+(1-b_{l})(1-b_{r})\left[x^{n}_{i}2p+(1-x^{n}_{i})(1-2p)2p\right].\end{split}

(54)

Since
f_{1}(x^{f}_{i},x^{n}_{i})
and
f_{2}(x^{f}_{i},x^{n}_{i})
are linear with respect to
x^{f}_{i}
and
x^{n}_{i}
, they are trivially continuous. Since
b_{l},b_{r}\in[0,1]
and
0\leq 2p<p+p^{f}<2p^{f}\leq 1
, we also find that
\frac{\partial f_{1}(x^{f}_{i},x^{n}_{i})}{\partial x^{f}_{i}},\frac{\partial f_{1}(x^{f}_{i},x^{n}_{i})}{\partial x^{n}_{i}},\frac{\partial f_{2}(x^{f}_{i},x^{n}_{i})}{\partial x^{f}_{i}},\frac{\partial f_{2}(x^{f}_{i},x^{n}_{i})}{\partial x^{n}_{i}}\in[0,1).
Thus, by applying the Banach fixed-point theorem, we know that as
i\to\infty
the fixed-point iteration
(x^{f}_{i+1},x^{n}_{i+1})=\left(f_{1}(x^{f}_{i},x^{n}_{i}),f_{2}(x^{f}_{i},x^{n}_{i})\right)
converges to the unique fixed point
(x^{f},x^{n})
such that
(x^{f},x^{n})=\left(f_{1}(x^{f},x^{n}),f_{2}(x^{f},x^{n})\right)
. This is a linear system of equations, the solutions of which provide the limiting values for
x^{f}
and
x^{n}
(see below in (
56
)).

We next compute
\mu(b_{l},b_{r})
using
x^{f}
and
x^{n}
. The asymptotic fraction of matched nodes is equal to the asymptotic probability that a random node
v^{r}_{i}\in V_{r}
is matched. If
v^{r}_{i}
is a flexible node, it is matched with
v^{l}_{i-1}
with probability
x^{f}_{i}
and matched with
v^{l}_{i}
with probability
(1-x^{f}_{i})\left[(1+b_{l})p^{f}+(1-b_{l})p\right]
. If it is a regular node, it is matched with
v^{l}_{i-1}
with probability
x^{n}_{i}
and matched with
v^{l}_{i}
with probability
(1-x^{n}_{i})\left[b_{l}p^{f}+(2-b_{l})p\right]
. Thus,

\begin{split}\mu(b_{l},b_{r})=&\;\lim_{i\to\infty}b_{r}x^{f}_{i}+b_{r}(1-x^{f}_{i})\left[(1+b_{l})p^{f}+(1-b_{l})p\right]+(1-b_{r})x^{n}_{i}+(1-b_{r})(1-x^{n}_{i})\left[b_{l}p^{f}+(2-b_{l})p\right]\\
=&\;b_{r}x^{f}+b_{r}(1-x^{f})\left[(1+b_{l})p^{f}+(1-b_{l})p\right]+(1-b_{r})x^{n}+(1-b_{r})(1-x^{n})\left[b_{l}p^{f}+(2-b_{l})p\right].\end{split}

(55)

Solving
x^{f}
and
x^{n}
from (
53
)-(
54
) and plugging into (
55
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mu(b_{l},b_{r})=\Biggl(

\displaystyle 2(b_{l}+b_{r})^{2}p^{4}b_{l}^{2}-2(b_{l}+b_{r})^{2}p^{4}b_{l}-8(b_{l}+b_{r})^{2}p^{3}p^{f}b_{l}^{2}+8(b_{l}+b_{r})^{2}p^{3}p^{f}b_{l}+12(b_{l}+b_{r})^{2}p^{2}\left(p^{f}\right)^{2}b_{l}^{2}

\displaystyle-12(b_{l}+b_{r})^{2}p^{2}\left(p^{f}\right)^{2}b_{l}-(b_{l}+b_{r})^{2}p^{2}-8(b_{l}+b_{r})^{2}p\left(p^{f}\right)^{3}b_{l}^{2}+8(b_{l}+b_{r})^{2}p\left(p^{f}\right)^{3}b_{l}+2(b_{l}+b_{r})^{2}pp^{f}

\displaystyle+2(b_{l}+b_{r})^{2}\left(p^{f}\right)^{4}b_{l}^{2}-2(b_{l}+b_{r})^{2}\left(p^{f}\right)^{4}b_{l}-(b_{l}+b_{r})^{2}\left(p^{f}\right)^{2}-4(b_{l}+b_{r})p^{4}b_{l}^{3}+2(b_{l}+b_{r})p^{4}b_{l}^{2}

\displaystyle+2(b_{l}+b_{r})p^{4}b_{l}+16(b_{l}+b_{r})p^{3}p^{f}b_{l}^{3}-8(b_{l}+b_{r})p^{3}p^{f}b_{l}^{2}-8(b_{l}+b_{r})p^{3}p^{f}b_{l}-24(b_{l}+b_{r})p^{2}\left(p^{f}\right)^{2}b_{l}^{3}

\displaystyle+12(b_{l}+b_{r})p^{2}\left(p^{f}\right)^{2}b_{l}^{2}+12(b_{l}+b_{r})p^{2}\left(p^{f}\right)^{2}b_{l}-2(b_{l}+b_{r})p^{2}b_{l}+7(b_{l}+b_{r})p^{2}+16(b_{l}+b_{r})p\left(p^{f}\right)^{3}b_{l}^{3}

\displaystyle-8(b_{l}+b_{r})p\left(p^{f}\right)^{3}b_{l}^{2}-8(b_{l}+b_{r})p\left(p^{f}\right)^{3}b_{l}+4(b_{l}+b_{r})pp^{f}b_{l}-6(b_{l}+b_{r})pp^{f}-2(b_{l}+b_{r})p

\displaystyle-4(b_{l}+b_{r})\left(p^{f}\right)^{4}b_{l}^{3}+2(b_{l}+b_{r})\left(p^{f}\right)^{4}b_{l}^{2}+2(b_{l}+b_{r})\left(p^{f}\right)^{4}b_{l}-2(b_{l}+b_{r})\left(p^{f}\right)^{2}b_{l}-(b_{l}+b_{r})\left(p^{f}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle+2(b_{l}+b_{r})p^{f}+2p^{4}b_{l}^{4}-2p^{4}b_{l}^{2}-8p^{3}p^{f}b_{l}^{4}+8p^{3}p^{f}b_{l}^{2}+12p^{2}\left(p^{f}\right)^{2}b_{l}^{4}-12p^{2}\left(p^{f}\right)^{2}b_{l}^{2}

\displaystyle+2p^{2}b_{l}^{2}-8p^{2}-8p\left(p^{f}\right)^{3}b_{l}^{4}+8p\left(p^{f}\right)^{3}b_{l}^{2}-4pp^{f}b_{l}^{2}+4p+2\left(p^{f}\right)^{4}b_{l}^{4}-2\left(p^{f}\right)^{4}b_{l}^{2}+2\left(p^{f}\right)^{2}b_{l}^{2}\Biggr)/

\displaystyle\Biggl(

\displaystyle(b_{l}+b_{r})^{2}p^{4}b_{l}^{2}-(b_{l}+b_{r})^{2}p^{4}b_{l}-4(b_{l}+b_{r})^{2}p^{3}p^{f}b_{l}^{2}+4(b_{l}+b_{r})^{2}p^{3}p^{f}b_{l}+6(b_{l}+b_{r})^{2}p^{2}\left(p^{f}\right)^{2}b_{l}^{2}

\displaystyle-6(b_{l}+b_{r})^{2}p^{2}\left(p^{f}\right)^{2}b_{l}-4(b_{l}+b_{r})^{2}p\left(p^{f}\right)^{3}b_{l}^{2}+4(b_{l}+b_{r})^{2}p\left(p^{f}\right)^{3}b_{l}+(b_{l}+b_{r})^{2}\left(p^{f}\right)^{4}b_{l}^{2}

\displaystyle-(b_{l}+b_{r})^{2}\left(p^{f}\right)^{4}b_{l}-2(b_{l}+b_{r})p^{4}b_{l}^{3}+(b_{l}+b_{r})p^{4}b_{l}^{2}+(b_{l}+b_{r})p^{4}b_{l}+8(b_{l}+b_{r})p^{3}p^{f}b_{l}^{3}-4(b_{l}+b_{r})p^{3}p^{f}b_{l}^{2}

\displaystyle-4(b_{l}+b_{r})p^{3}p^{f}b_{l}-12(b_{l}+b_{r})p^{2}\left(p^{f}\right)^{2}b_{l}^{3}+6(b_{l}+b_{r})p^{2}\left(p^{f}\right)^{2}b_{l}^{2}+6(b_{l}+b_{r})p^{2}\left(p^{f}\right)^{2}b_{l}-2(b_{l}+b_{r})p^{2}b_{l}

\displaystyle+3(b_{l}+b_{r})p^{2}+8(b_{l}+b_{r})p\left(p^{f}\right)^{3}b_{l}^{3}-4(b_{l}+b_{r})p\left(p^{f}\right)^{3}b_{l}^{2}-4(b_{l}+b_{r})p\left(p^{f}\right)^{3}b_{l}+4(b_{l}+b_{r})pp^{f}b_{l}

\displaystyle-2(b_{l}+b_{r})pp^{f}-2(b_{l}+b_{r})\left(p^{f}\right)^{4}b_{l}^{3}+(b_{l}+b_{r})\left(p^{f}\right)^{4}b_{l}^{2}+(b_{l}+b_{r})\left(p^{f}\right)^{4}b_{l}-2(b_{l}+b_{r})\left(p^{f}\right)^{2}b_{l}

\displaystyle-(b_{l}+b_{r})\left(p^{f}\right)^{2}+p^{4}b_{l}^{4}-p^{4}b_{l}^{2}-4p^{3}p^{f}b_{l}^{4}+4p^{3}p^{f}b_{l}^{2}+6p^{2}\left(p^{f}\right)^{2}b_{l}^{4}-6p^{2}\left(p^{f}\right)^{2}b_{l}^{2}+2p^{2}b_{l}^{2}-4p^{2}

\displaystyle-4p\left(p^{f}\right)^{3}b_{l}^{4}+4p\left(p^{f}\right)^{3}b_{l}^{2}-4pp^{f}b_{l}^{2}+\left(p^{f}\right)^{4}b_{l}^{4}-\left(p^{f}\right)^{4}b_{l}^{2}+2\left(p^{f}\right)^{2}b_{l}^{2}+1\Biggr).

(56)

Given the closed-form expression for
\mu(b_{l},b_{r})
in (
56
), to prove
Theorem

7.1
it suffices to compute
\mu(B,0)-\mu(B/2,B/2)
and verify that the difference is strictly positive. Using Wolfram Mathematica in
Theorem2.nb
,
16
16

16

The codes can be found at
https://bit.ly/3uS7wW6
.
we verify that
\mu(B,0)-\mu(B/2,B/2)>0
for all
B\in(0,1]
and
0\leq p<p^{f}\leq~1/2
.
\square

F.2
Proof of
Theorem

C.2

For the concavity result, we evaluate
\nabla_{(0,1)}^{2}\mu(b_{l},b_{r})
with
b_{l}=1/2
, which is equivalent to
\frac{\partial^{2}\mu(1/2,b_{r})}{\partial b_{r}^{2}}
. In
Theorem6.nb
,
17
17

17

The codes can be found at
https://bit.ly/3uRh6bQ
.
we again use Wolfram Mathematica to verify that
\frac{\partial^{2}\mu(1/2,b_{r})}{\partial b_{r}^{2}}<0
for all
b_{r}\in(0,1)
and
0\leq p<p^{f}\leq 1/2
. The case for the direction
(1,0)
is symmetric. The proof for
\frac{\partial^{2}\mu(0,b_{r})}{\partial b_{r}^{2}}<0
for all
b_{r}\in(0,1)
and
0\leq p<p^{f}\leq 1/2
follows from the same analyses by taking
b_{l}=0
.

The convexity result is easier to analyze by hands. We set
b_{r}=1-b_{l}
to simplify (
56
) to

\mu(b_{l},1-b_{l})=2\frac{\left(p^{f}\right)^{2}b_{l}^{2}-\left(p^{f}\right)^{2}b_{l}-2p^{f}pb_{l}^{2}+2p^{f}pb_{l}+p^{f}+p^{2}b_{l}^{2}-p^{2}b_{l}+p}{\left(p^{f}\right)^{2}b_{l}^{2}-\left(p^{f}\right)^{2}b_{l}-2p^{f}pb_{l}^{2}+2p^{f}pb_{l}+p^{f}+p^{2}b_{l}^{2}-p^{2}b_{l}+p+1}.

Taking the second-order derivative with respect to
b_{l}
, we obtain

\frac{\partial^{2}\mu(b_{l},1-b_{l})}{\partial b_{l}^{2}}=\frac{4\left(p^{f}-p\right)^{2}\underbrace{\left[-\left(p^{f}\right)^{2}-3b_{l}^{2}\left(p^{f}-p\right)^{2}+3b_{l}\left(p^{f}-p\right)^{2}+2p^{f}p+p^{f}-p^{2}+p+1\right]}_{(I)}}{\left(\underbrace{b_{l}^{2}\left(p^{f}-p\right)^{2}-b_{l}\left(p^{f}-p\right)^{2}+p^{f}+p+1}_{II}\right)^{3}}.

We demonstrate that both
(I)
and
(II)
are strictly positive for all
b_{l}\in(0,1)
and
0\leq p<p^{f}\leq 1/2
, ensuring
\frac{\partial^{2}\mu(b_{l},1-b_{l})}{\partial b_{l}^{2}}>0
. For
(I)
we have

\displaystyle(I)=

\displaystyle\;3b_{l}(1-b_{l})\left(p^{f}-p\right)^{2}-\left(p^{f}-p\right)^{2}+p^{f}+p+1

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\left(3b_{l}-3b_{l}^{2}-1\right)\left(p^{f}-p\right)^{2}+p^{f}+p+1

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle-\left(p^{f}-p\right)^{2}+p^{f}+p+1\geq-0.25+p^{f}+p+1>0,

where the first inequality comes from
b_{l}-b_{l}^{2}\geq 0
and the second from
\left(p^{f}-p\right)^{2}\leq 0.25.

For
(II)
we have

(II)=(b_{l}^{2}-b_{l})\left(p^{f}-p\right)^{2}+p^{f}+p+1\geq-0.25+p^{f}+p+1>0,

where the first inequality comes from
b_{l}^{2}-b_{l}\geq-1
and
\left(p^{f}-p\right)^{2}\leq 0.25.
(since
p^{f}\leq 1/2
). Since
\nabla_{(1,-1)}^{2}\mu(b_{l},b_{r})
with
b_{l}+b_{r}=1
is equivalent to
\frac{\partial^{2}\mu(b_{l},1-b_{l})}{\partial b_{l}^{2}}>0
, we conclude the proof.
\square

Appendix G
Additional Simulation Results

In this section, we consider an imbalanced model that extends the spatial model in
Section

7.2
. Assuming that there are
n
drivers and
\lambda\cdot n
riders, we again find that such an imbalance strengthens the case for the one-sided allocation because the allocation
(B,0)
creates more flexible agents (in expectation) than any other allocation. As illustrated in
Fig.

34
(a) and (b), this makes the better of the one-sided allocations outperform the balanced allocation for any sufficiently large
\alpha
and
\alpha^{f}
, regardless of whether
B<1
. At the same time, we still observe the balanced allocation outperforming the one-sided allocation in the regime with very small
\alpha,\alpha^{f}
, where the number of edges is much larger for the balanced allocation (see
Section

7.2
).

(a)
B=0.6

(b)
B=1

Figure 34:
Values of
\frac{\mu^{\texttt{EMP}}(B/2,B/2)}{\max\left\{\mu^{\texttt{EMP}}(B,0),\mu^{\texttt{EMP}}(0,B)\right\}}
across varying
\alpha
and
\alpha^{f}-\alpha
when
\lambda=0.8.
